WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Duke of Richmond and his Seats.—The Prince and Princess of Wales will spend the most of the remain- , lug time in Scotland, before their departure for India, • and the Prince will, next month, be the guest of the, Duke of Richmond at Gordon Castle, for a few days salmon fishing on the Spey. Gordon Castle is one of the largest houses in Scotland, with a huge square ' tower in the centre, but it is not kept up inith all the splendour of former times. or even is Goodwood Park, where the Duke recently entertained the King and Queen, and a large circle of guests for race week. Both his Sussex and Banffshire seats are expensive 'places to keep up,and the Duke of Richmond is not as fortunately placed as many of his fellow peers, whcse property in ; London or urban districts has so greatly increased in 'value, as to more than compensate for reduced agricul- tural rents. It was no doubt because of the pressure of reduced rents that the Duke sold the line family mansion in London, which is now occupied by a South African millionaire.

The spelling and Pronunciation of Family Names.— Coke is one of the many family names that are spelt one way and pronounced another. It is always pronounced "Cook." There is, however, not so much variance in this as there is in many other surnames, such as Choi- mondley, which becomes Chumley ; Colquhon, Coboon : Marjoribanks, Marchbanks; Carew, Cairy ; Buchan, Buckan ; Claverho use, Claverse ; Abergavenny, Aber- genny ; and so forth. The list might be extended indefinitely, though why people should take a pride in pronunciations so hopelessly at variance with spellings, is not very obvious, in view of the fact that the custom no doubt originated for much the same reason that the street hawkers' cry of "old clothes" is turned into "ole do." Mark Twain once inquired _why such a logical people as we claimed to be should tolerate such an anomaly, but he was told that it must be much on the same principle that made" Samuel Clemens " sound the same as if it were spelt" Mark Twain." Practical Philanthrophy.—Lady St. Helier, until recently Lady Jeune is giving up her town and country establishments, and intends in the future to devote most of her time to the welfare of the poorer classes. She has sold Arlington Manor, in Berkshire, where she and the late Lord St. Helier used to entertain a great deal, and is also giving up her house in Harley street. Lady St. Helier has often written striking articles on the social problems of the moment and has always been deeply interested in charitable and philanthropic under- takings for the benefit of the poor. Even in her husband's lifetime she was well known in the East End of London, and has now taken a house in one of the I poorest anl busiest parts of the metropolis, where she proposes to carry on her work for the improvement relevation of the poorest classes. Lady St. Helier be- lieves thoroughly in the doctrine that if you want a thing done, you must do it yourself, and she is showing I in a very practical way that she is in earnest in her efforts to better the condition of the poor and uncared for.

The Picture Post Card.—At no time of the year does the picture post card play a more useful part than during the holiday season. No doubt its popularity depends to some extent upon its frivolity, as many people spend a great deal of their time in hunting out and purchasing cards with satirical suggestions relating to the hobbies or weaknesses of their friends to whom the cards are sent. It is a harmless way of providing fan for the breakfast table, but the great merit of the picture card is, that it enables us to keep on good terms with our friends with out making a burden of one's correspondence. The old-fashioned letter of several pages closely written, and crossed, has in fact almost disappeared, except perhaps amongst the community of old maiden aunts. With the great majority of people the picture card serves sufficiently for all epistolary requirements, and our thanks are due to the person who tint invented this very useful means of saving us time and trouble, About Luggage.—The holidays are making it very clear that, as a nation, we have still further improved in the matter of luggage. The battered tin box is an item of the past, neat leather portmanteaux, or Glad- stone bags of wicker covered in canvas, having taken its place. Then there are the useful expanding wicker baskets, into which an almost incredible amount of clothing can be packed. Elaborately fitting dressing bags are rarely seen nowadays, the unfortunnte owners having discovered that the majority of the fittings are more or less useless, and that when packed such a bag requires a man and a boy to lift it. The Safest Place in a Train.—With the holiday season in full swing, there is usually not much choice as re- • gards any particular seat in a train. The carriages are so crowded that it is usually a case of getting in where one can, and there is no opportunity to consider which is the safest seat on the train. The question, however, is often asked by travellers, and an expert gives it as his opinion after careful calculation, that the safest place is in the middle of the last car but one. There are some risks he says which are the same in every part of the train, but others which are less at this particular spot. It is not of course always possible to pick and choose, but those who are nervous about rail- v;aytravelling might bear in mind that a centre seat is safer than a corner one and so also is a centre com- partment, as compared with the end compartment of a car._ _ .

Flowers Fashionable.—Corsage bouquets are worn all :day by smart society women, the morning " knot " being small and simple, that donned in the afternoon 'being larger and more elaborate, while the evening corsage bouquet is of truly generous proportions, and most exquisitely arranged. During the past few weeks, the sweet pea has been the favourite flower,indeed there has been an absolute craze for it, but its place is now beiag taken by the carnation, and gardenia. The Craze for White.—This has been a white summer, and there are not wanting signs that it will be a white autumn and in all probability a white winter too. At the moment, white cloth serge, alpaca and hopsack, are enloying an almost unprecedented demand, costumes built of these fabrics being usually either trimmed with strappings of the same, motifs of white silk passe- menterie, or very narrow gold braid. The last-named is of course the most effective, but for wear at the sea- side, it is not to be recommended as it tarnishes so quickly. The bolero description of coat is perhaps first in favour, but the little sac and reefer varieties are much worn. The long redingote is very smart looking when worn by tall, slender women, but only this type should indulge in this particular garment. Autumn Millinery.—The French sailor, in Dunstable straw, panama, or felt is having a great vogue. A neat band of velvet, or a twisted silk scarf is the us‘al trimming, the hat being tilted well forward over the face, and finished of at the back with a pair of wings. Those among us who are desirous of postponing the purchase of new millinery should go catefully over hats and toques, remove all faded and crushed trim- mings, and add a touch of velvet, and where suitable, wings. Birds and ospreys, will strictly be avoided by all those women who regard the fashion from a humanitarian point of view. BIADGE.

THE FORESIGHT _OF JAPAN.

The admirable faculty of the Japanese for making pro- vision for the future event never has been more strikingly illustrated than in the care which they are taking to make themselves acquainted with the methods of our fishermen on the Dogger Bank. Mr. Kata is the name of the Japanese official to whom this inspection of our fisheries is entrusted, and the main motive of the inspection is said to be that the Japanese may be able to make the best possible use of the island of Saghalien as a fishing station so soon as it falls completely under their control ; for the purpose of a fishing station it is said to be admirably adapted. That it will fall into the absolute possession of Japan is an event that the Japanese themselves evidently do not doubt. At the same time, it is very doubtful whether many people in the West would have so much provision as thus to study the arts of peace in preparation for the time when the 'arts of war may be abandoned in their favour.— Country Life."

The Corporation of Glasgow never fails to make good rise of its balances. and with over £77,000 to the good, £287,437 has been expended in meeting interest on capital, paying income-tax, Parliamentary expenses, etc., besides making the annual payment to the fund "known as the Common Good, a term which explains itself.

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

THE EMPME COAT.

The revival of the Empire coat has been quite one of the events of the summer. No one ex- pected it, but now it has arrived everybody wants it, while the fact that it is not to be found among the ready-made wraps very considerably enhances its desirability in the eyes of the Not that there is anything seriously complicated in the construction of the Empire coat: but wherever there is an emphatic line, more especially one with any reference to the waist, there is a certain amount of individual fitting, necessitated. And fitting is just the one detail the manufacturers, who turn out things by the gross, cannot contemplate. This is why, and the whole and solo reason why, the close-fitting back still hangs fire, and the loose sacque pursues the even tenour of its wearisome way. Apparently a firm stand has been made by the whole modistic fraternity against being trammelled in the slightest degree over the vogue, since, apart from the short-waisted outline, the most un- orthodox revers, collars, or absence of both, to- gether with positively outrageous sleeves, are not merely permissible but form a distinctive momento of the hour. YOUNG GIRL'S FROCK.

This exceedingly simple yet smart little frock was worn by a pretty girl of about sixteen. It was carried out in a red-and-white checked zephyr. The blouse fastened behind, and the material was laid in deep folds over the shoulders, narrowing slightly towards the waist. The folds were ornamented and kept in place with French knots worked in black. The sleeves were pleated in at the shoulder, and the material arranged in folds below the elbow, and trimmed like the biota*, with French knots. The full

skirt was eased in at the waist, and trimmed at the hem with two flat folds of the material. The neck and wrists were finished with a dainty collar and cuffs of broderie Anglaise ; and a scarf of black silk was worn,which was passed through large buttonholes in the front of the blouse. A belt of white kid completed this charming frock.

MRS. ALMERIC PAGET.

Fate has a funny way of tempering her favours with drawbacks. Her latest victim is Mrs. Almeric Paget, who came into an enor- mous fortune not long aglb and who purposed entertaining on a very large scale this season. Scarcely had she made her plans when she was laid up with a very serious illness, at one time believed to be of the nature of diphtheria. Fortunately she so far recovered as to ho able to have one or two parties early in the season, but her doctors said she would have to go away for a rest as soon as possible. She determined to remain in town till practically the end of the season before going abroad. Hitherto Mrs. Almeric Paget has had her fair share of luck. She is a very handsome woman with a fine car- riage; in tact, a typical American beauty, full of energy and vivacity, and a very clever and entertaining talker. Her father, the gossips say, left about £10,000,000, and of this I am told she takes a tenth share in her own right. Her husband, too, is now a wealthy man though in his early days he was not overburdened with this world's goods.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
A SHAWL CAPE.

The subject of our illustration is a pretty Cos- tume, consisting of a shawl cape worn with a skirt to match. These shawl capes are very graceful, and are cut to resemble a shawl. For present wear, we would suggest a loosely-woven canvas cloth in a rather light shade of brown. The cape reaches to below the knees in front, and behind it is cut to a point, and reaches to about, the waist line. It is outlined with three rows of brown ribbon velvet, graduating in

width, and the collar is also faced with brown velvet. The fronts of the cape are left open a few inches, to shew the pretty cream lace blouse worn beneath. Two ornamental fastenings of brown silk braid just hold the cape in position. The deep belt should be of brown silk exactly matching the material. The full skirt flows in graceful lines to the hem, and is trimmed with three rows of ribbon velvet. TAKE CARE OF YOUR DIGESTION.

Girls who want to be attractive and who con- sider an attractive body an essential for a woman, begin by making themselves healthy. In- stead of working on their ccmplexions they work upon their digestion, knowing that as soon as the digestive organs are in good shape the com- plexion will ,take care of itself. As soon as the stomach is properly fed, the blood will be healthier and the skin will glow with the bril- liancy of youth.

TO-DAT'S RECIPi.

MEDALLIONS A LA CAVENDISIL—Pound about 12oz. of scraped, fresh whiting, add 3oz. of butter, 4oz. of panada, 2 whole eggs, and the yolks of two others. When the ingredients are well mixed, rub through a fine wire sieve and mix with half a gill of very thick cream, a tablespoonful of passed lobster spawn, and pepper and salt to taste. Spread the fish on a buttered saute-pan, cover it with a greased paper, and cook it in a moderate oven until it is firm to the touch. When the fish is quite cold, cut it into pieces with a round cutter, mask them with chaudfroid sauce made with the re- duced 'jailor from the fish bones, season nicely. and colour pink with passed lobster spawn. Put a round piece of truffle on the centre of *ach, and dish them on a border of aspic jelly. Garnish the centre with scallops of lobster rnized with thick liliMCO% • Warmie...n

'ALL SPOILED HUSBANDS-AND ,

(Author of " Sooty," " 7e Jesters," se.).

uoa nusband is " born not made," even as is the poet, and like most good things he is rare. Unfortimately, too, he is seldom success- fully meted. Any observant looker-on at the domestic unities will have noticed that an exemplary, patient and moral man is invariably attached to a shrewish, impetuous, and exactig woman ! That one of high intellectual gifts has for a mate a foolish or wordly-minded person to whom the material things of life ap- peal more than the intellectual. Again a modest and contended disposition is frequently allied to a restless and ambitious one, thus seeming to prove that man and woman are attracted by the very opposite qualities to those they individually possess. _ . .

Marriage is a veritable lottery. Its prizes are few in cmparison with its blanks. The poetic theory of the twin souls rarely outlasts the engagement stage. Every week of every month of the trst year of marriage is a stage of disillusion—cm attempt to re-adjust ideals and ideas to the hard, cold prose of every-day existence to reconcile the devoted; patient, all perfect lover to the mere ordinary man, who flies into an ordinary human rage if breakfast is late—if his razor has proved inefficient—or his morning paper is not on the table, and bills— are ! Divided by a great gulf indeed ore the romance and passion of love from the common- place exactions of marriage.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
WREN THE YOKE GILLS.

-It is hard to believe, but it is a fact. There- fore to ensure something like content and happi- ness in the marriage state it is better to chose a companion whose tastes and temper suit your own ; who has something -better than mere personal charm, or physical beauty, on which to depend. There are some fitted by nature tt, bear the yoke of matrimony, and there are others to whom it never can and never will Ira anything but a yoke—a burden—a harass—an irritant. From the first moment they realise bondage instead of freedom, exactions and obli- gations instead of latitude, they grow restive, and their wives have a bad time of it. The wise woman recognises this sense of restiveness, and instead of opposing,. yields to it as far as pos- sible. It is not so much a question of the latch- key and the club as it is of the non-inteiference with certain habits that have become second nature—the ceasing to impose restrictions when they chafe and annoy and are really not re- quired. It would be well for women to re- member that men in their anti-married state have been accustomed to perfect freedom : a certain lawless liberty of action. They cannot at once give up their habits—and this sense of freedom. And, indeed, why should they be expected to do so? Taken sensibly and with good humour, the so-called " liberty " often shows itself very harmless if it is not interfered with. Above all it enables a woman to demand the same for her- self. She recognises that her husband has a life apart from the dual obligations of marriage —and by such recognition renders home a pleasant retreat—an agreeable interlude. Such a wife proves herself a good comrade—and an intelligent companion, and as the years go on they bring her a rich reward. It is not unusual to hear one woman say to another "Oh ! you spoil your husband ! I wouldn't let mine do such and such a thing !" Such a remark is sufficient guarantee of that household. It is eloquent of hen-pecking, squabbles—disagreements, and—most vital of all—the struggle for mastery which too often embitters home-life, and estranges hearts that once vowed love, honour, and truth to each other.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BIT, THE CURE, IRE BEARING REIN.

There are many inen who are spoilt as hus- bands by the mere fact of being tied down to discipline and bonclac.e. There are others who are all the better for °being taken in band by a strong mid capable wife, mid taught the true meaning of duty and responsibility. But with that curious evidence of Some marplet at work with human fate, rarely do these cases unite. The husband who requires managing invariably falls to the lot of the one woman in the world incapable of understanding his necessity—and the man who has no talent for domestic life or need of it, takes to himself a shrew or a fury who makes of that life a hideous travesty of what it ought to be, and would be in different and more capable lianas ! The extraordinary number of ill-matched couples one daily meets, makes one wonder where the real attraction of marriage lies? What sudden' and injudicious frenzy induces people to rush into an alliance that—in a very few years —proves to be unsuitable. How is it they are so blind before—so keen-sighted after- wards ? And why, also, is it that a woman is so unutterably foolish as not to appreciate a man who has burdened himself with her welfare and support, when he might be a free-mid-easy and. irresponsible bachelor ! Who is kind—and good-tempered and generous —but who has a few human faults as well—and does not ap- preciate their perpetual se-capitulation. Why do so many women spoil men even as they spoil horses by too lavish use of spur and whip and bearing rein? Why do they exact so much, and yet show so little gratitude for what needs no exaction! The proverb says "A young man married is a man that's marred," and in nine cases out of ten this is true, for to often a young man marries in haste and in hot-headed passion the very woman who in later years -he would never dream of making his wife. Again in early marriages, the young man invariably gives in to his wife in everything. Thus—when years of sense and disillusion come upon him, he is bound hand and foot. A weak slave to an unsparing tyrant—who never can be brought to recognise her tyranny, but takes rule as her right and is never happier than when making the man he should honour appear a fool in the eyes of her friends. Here, indeed, is a spoiled husband who will never perhaps get a chance of proving what grit is really in his nature—and would have shown itself under 'different circumstances.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MABRIAGE OF SONVENIENCH.

But as there are many husbands spoiled by tyranny and selfishness so there are also mmi- bers ruined by weakness, over-petting, and over-consideration ; to whom the wife is an abject slave—a creature to use and abuse as the fancy takes them—the servile ministrant to domestic comfort—a part of the goods and chattels of the house; never considered, never appreciated, and only missed because of her uses. Men of this disposition merely marry for their own convenience. They are incapable of a true, unselfish devotion, and the more a woman yields the more they exact. It is an odd fact that their wives never seem to realise the actual Selfishness of such husbands as these, but are quite content to slave for them, pamper them, study their every wish and martyrize their own bodies and souls in an Unthankful service.

If the dance of life could only bring about a change of partners as does the last figure of the Lancers, what a delightful and unexpected re- adjustment of evils there would be ! The mild, sweet-tempered man might secure the gentle, self-saciilicing wife ot the tyrant ! The shrewish, imperative • lalanan find herself confronted by e exacting and selfish Benedict. Intellect —and mental appreciation of its possessor, might clasp hands in blissful content. Bril- liatte anti homeliness—wit and beauty—purity and chivalry—meet on neutral ground and take each their metal of human favour and compre- hension. For it is just the lack of comprehen- dioll that spoils so many lives. The inability to Understand ; to be patient with faults and kind to virtues. • The unpardonable offence of a blow to vanity —a sneer at defects—personal or mental, hare created wider havoc amongst the domesticities, -tt hie than even ill-usnge. A woman too often fed on flatteries by the lover to pardon the blunt truths of the • husband. Site cannot understand that having once been per- fect in his eyes, she should ever cease to possess perfection. His one unpardonable sin is com- mitted when he points out her defects instead of magnifying her good qualities. But, indeed, most men are equally vain with respect to their own virtues—and just as susceptible to flattery as a woman. There is, however, this dif- ference between their relative positions in , life. A man mixes with his fellow man early onongh to guage his own worth by their ap- proval. If his vanity or his weaka.e.Js persona, uener m filmset, tire narotien lay und.e appreciation from his wife, his content is often rudely disturbed by the harsher criticism of his fellows. In the rough and tumble battle of life there is an excellent cawing ground Lot

conceit. has a man gifts of person—ability— wit business capacity shrewdness even genius, so have others—many others. Let no man say to himself his place in the world can never be filled, his mark on its crowded pages never obliterated. " That which bath been shall be," says the wise preacher. Clear brains —wise minds—noble virtues—cunning inven- tions—the discoveries of Science—the • achiev- molts of Art—all have been even as all shall be. What is new under the suit? ===== 0002601_19050819_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
"'ACT AND SYMPATHY.

The paths of Fate are narrow, and circum- scribed. If two people choose one path and expect to walk abreast they speedily discover its limits. One of the twain must be pushed aside, or give place, or be left behind in the march. Sometimes the separation is but tem- porary. But very oftcn, it is long and lasting; bred of disagreement and intolerance ; the escape of a prisoner eager foi freedom—not the temporary giving place to a companion of the road.

Alas ! that, as the result of selection, such companionship should be so rare! That married life is too often a spoiled—patched—or harmful state and condition, instead of an evidence of happy union. That love so seldom mates with comprehension of itself. Of its infinite possi- bilities, desires, and exactions. That even genius of high order has left records of miseraole husbands—and misunderstood wives ! And this • because tact mid sympathy and comprehension have been lacking in either nature ; for apart even from love and devotion these qualities are of inestimable value. If they were joined to the love or devotion, perhaps the• marriage state would become too perfect for mere ordinary humanity! Something at once unspoilable and unspoilt.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR NOTES.

1 The Automobile Club is to be congratulated. Barely two months had elapsed since the South Harting Hill Climb took place before the handicap results were worked out and published. It is true that nobody is particularly interested in the matter, for the winners of each class were declared at the time whilst the actual times made by each car were withheld then as now, so that none of us are really much the wiser for the elaborate table which has just been given to the world. But, nevertheless, we are all greatly obliged to the Club for the trouble it has taken in the matter, and those of the competitors who have not disposed of their vehicles in order to buy more op-to-date cars will doubtless be gratified by seeing them mentioned, even though the real fact they may have wished to ascertain still remains a sealed mystery.

The Auto-Cycle Club, whose unique methods of arranging competitions for its members have already added considerably to the gaiety of nations, started thirty-one riders upon a six days' reliability trial, last Monday, and under circumstances which fully sustained its reputation in the matter of organisation. Ordinary traffic was entirely obstructed, bewildered officials, irate competitors, indignant pedestrians and noisily running engines all combining to make Down-street, Piccadilly, anything but a desirable locality during the half-hour's unexplained delay which preceded the start. Con- sidering that the competitors taking part in these trials pay a heavy fee for the questionable privilege, they surely have the right to expect that some effort at order will be made, and that organisation will at least be up to the standard always maintained by the Motor Cycling Club whose events are invariably so carefully controlled although all its officials are honorary.

A terrible fatality has occurred during the competition for the Ilerkomer Trophy, a Continental event in which England contributed a team. In round- ing a corner, we are told, one of the cars dashed into and killed two little children who had beet: obscured from the driver's view by an immense cloud of dust. In consequence of this, the German Automobile Club immediately issued a rule forbidding any competitor to take a corner at more than twenty miles an hour, under pain of disqualification. This regulation would be more reassuring if we wee told how every corner is to be protected, who is to decide between a "corner" and a curve," and how the time keeping and speed judging is to be accomplished.

This accident, of course, is but another illustration of the criminal folly of allowing high-speed contests upon any high road, and the sooner such are absolutely prohibited the better for the sport and the industry as well. But a word of warning with regard to taking corners and curves under ordinary circumstances will not be out of place here. A good driver always slows down for these and also de-clutches. He slows down because he cannot tell what is on the further side of I that corner nor how he may be involved even though he take his turn on his right side, and he de-clutches because, otherwise the pull upon his tyre is tremendous since a dragging sideways strain is thus added to the driving one. fyres are in themselves sufficiently unreliable and short-lived without their brief existence being still further curtailed by poor driving, and motorists will do well to bear this in mind.

Motorists will be interested to note that suspension wheels were fitted to the winning car in the race over the Ardennes Circuit. Undoubtedly, these are, by far, the stronger type, as we have so constantly pointed out in this column; but public prejudice has long favoured those of the "artillery" pattern. In this matter, indeed, the makers have been guilty of a step backwards, for the early motor cars seen in this country were always fitted with "wire" wheels, and it was certainly a retro- grade movement to adopt the wooden substitutes. With the exception of the Humberette, the Rover, and one or two other small cars, and the Lanchester in the large car class, artillery wheels have been universally used, but the appearance of two successful racers equipped with the mechanically correct type, coupled with the recollection of more than one fatal accident directly attributed to the collapse of the wooden wheels, may be the means of influencing a return to the suspen- sion pattern.

THE LEGEND OF WINWICK.

A Probable Explanation.

anTdhiverefroclulorwy Lnginathppeeacrosuirnsethoef a"nLairvteirepleooolul?,asiulyrviPvionsgt Superstitions " : Three miles on this side of Warrington stands the ancient Winwick Church. On the west side of the tower, some thirty or forty feet from its base, there is a roughly-carved pig. Now the good people of that dis- trict claim that King Oswald fell and died not at Oswestry, but at Winwick. Is not his honoured name also perpetuated in that of the present rector, Canon Oswald Penrhyn ? The legend goes that, desiring to erect a church to the memory of the Christian King on the spot where he fell fighting against the Pagan King Penda, A.D. 642,the people began to collect hewn stones. But every morning the stones already placed there ready for the foundations were found to have been moved during the night several yards away. This continued for a week or so. One night a watch was kept, and a pig was observed rolling the heavy stones, and as he went he grunted " Win-wick, Win-wik I" Hence the name of the village and also the caning on the tower. The people felt that the pig had been inspired to show them the true spot where Oswald fell. The truth of the matter, however, is probably found in the fact that it was customary in early times, in order to avert the anger of the gods and to insure the safety of the building, to wall up a i living human being n the foundations with other offer- ings. In process of time a live animal was substituted, also with offerings. To-day we place under the founda- tion-stone a bottle of wine and coin of th. 9 realm. The latter is our offering, and the wine, spilled on the breaking of the bottle, represents the spilling of human or animal blood. In small buildings an old horseshoe is sometimes laid in the wall for luck, or a piece of money hidden in the mortar for the same reason.

OLD SORES ARE WORST.

A Mutiny Veteran Praises Zam-Buk.

When the skin has once been injured or diseased especially when any germ or poison has settled in the tissues, trouble often occurs in after life. Sufferers trona ulcers of any description may 'learn from the experience of an Indian Mutiny veteran, Mr. Robert Horton, of 27, Red Laver-street, Cardiff, how Zam-Buk will eradicate the worst complaint. "At the storming of Taku Fort, China, in 1860, I was :shot in the ankle," said Mr. Horton to a "Cardiff Times" reporter, "and though I was only invalided for a fort- night, I always had cause to remember the wound. Twenty years later, while working at the docks here, I had the misfortune to break the skin over it, and the place being neglected, got worse and worse. In -fact, from that day to a couple of months ago, I had a running wound at the spot the whole time. Erysipelas -and blood-poisoning set in,and I had to go into hospital on four separate occasions and lose a lot of time at my work. I tried, I should say, every ointment, oil, and liniment supposed to cure complaints of this kind, and I took blood medicines of various descriptions. Although 130 Me remedies kept my ankle from getting worse, and alleviated the agony to some extent, there was always the open wound, which at times was the size of half-a- crown. "Five or six months ago I read of the astonishing cures effected by Zam-Buk, and gave the balm a trial. Although I have had to use many boxes of it, I must say that I noticed an improvement after the first box. I -soon found the skin around the wound getting white and healthy-looking, so I kept on dressing it with Zam- Buk regularly. Now lam glad to say that the wound has dried up and that the skin has completely healed. Although such a ripe old age. I am now able to follow ray occupation without any trouble whatsoever." Zam-Buk is a powerful remedy for obstinate sores, bad legs, diseased ankles. running wounds, inflamma- tion, peeling or scaling skin, scurvy, sore backs,poisoned or festering sores, erysipelas, piles, eczema, psoriasis, scalp diseases, barber's rash, abscesses, boils, ringworm, sore breasts, chafed skin, sunburn, nettlerash, heat spots, freckles, blisters, evuptions due to acid or exces- sive perspiration, stiffness, &c. Price Is. lid. or 2s. 9d. per box (2s. 9d. sin containing nearly four times the Is. Id.) of all chemists, or the Zam-Buk Co„ 4, Red Cross-street, London, E.C.

VOLUNTEERS' RETURN. vivis Closing Incidents of the Salisbury Plain Camp, Looking bronzed and the pictures of health the ; War. ington Volunteers returned home on Saturday evening after a fortnight's encampment on Salisbury Plain. The training has beau entered into with the best possible spirit by the men and cannot fail to have a good effect on the physique and efficiency of the ' battalion. The last night in camp was made the occasion of a smoking concert promoted by the sergeants, to which the sergeants of the ; other battalions in the brigade were invited. Sergt.-Major Ingram presided, and was supported by I many of the officers of the battalion. He apologized $ for the absence of Colonel Edelsten, who had been ' invited by General Sir lan Hamilton to dine with the Duke of Connaught at Tidworth House. A capital pro- , gramme of songs, &c., was given by Sergts. Walker, Lloyd, Taylor, Lawless, and Richardson, Mr. G. H. Bright, Mr. Clune,Capt. Roberts, Lce-Corpl, Glover, Sergt. Jones, (Ist Herefords)' LINA. J. Picton, Sergt. Massey. Mr.Fairclougih, Mr. Gautier, Sergt. Rigby and Sergt. Evan Jones (Ist Royal Welsh). The health of the officers was proposed by Col.-Sergt. Wood, Surgeon. Major Adams and Capt. Reg. Smith responding. On Saturday morning the volunteers were up before I five o'clock preparing to strike tents and pack all the bagirage ready for the return home. All the tents were racked in bags' and the blankets and other camp ecpiipace placed in piles ready for handing over to the I Army Ordnance Department. These duties were in the •case of the South Lancashires under the supervision of Captain and Quartermaster Potter, who, with his assist- I ant, Quartermaster-Sergeant Willcocks, has had charge i of the commisariat department. Between ten and eleven I in the awning three trains were despatched from Lodger 11 for Warrington. The arrival of the battalion at Warrington was witnessod by large crowds, the streets from the Central Station to the Drill Hall being lined with spectators. 'The first train, which reached Warrington at about half- past six, brought in B, C, G, H and D Companies, together with the commanding offices, the adjutant, and also the members of the band. The second train arrived about an hour later, and comprized A, E, F and M.l. Companies, and the bugle band, while the third train contained the cyclists and transports, und arrived at Warrington shortly before eight o'clock. The men • marched along Horsemarket-street and Sankey-street to the Park, after which they went to the Drill Hall and dispersed. A critic of the "Daily Telegraph," who, writing of ! the work of the South Lancashires and Cheshires, says: I —There is a great improvement noticeable already in the knowledge and style of the Auxiliary force. Two or three battalions in particular stand out as being remarkably good. These are the second V.B. Cheshire Regiment, and the Ist V.B. Lancashire Regiment. In point of drill they could hold their own with the average line battalion, and the Cheshires in the matter of physique, could give points to any regular battalion in the army. On Wednesday when going for the day's work, they paraded in their trousers and shirts, with sleeves rolled up to the elbow, and a sturdier or more workmanlike body of soldiers it would be impossible to desire. A group of officers were watching them passing headquarters, and one remarked that they must he regulars. As they had no tunics on of course the distinguishing badge was absent. His neighbour replied, "Indeed they're not. There isn't a line battalion with physique like that in the service."

WARRINGTON BURIAL SOCIETY.

Annual Meeting. The members of the above Burial Society met at the British Workman Public House. Horse market-street, onpfenday to celebrate the 35th anniversary of the establishment of the Society. The chair was occupied by the president Mr. Joseph Ashton, who was supported on the platform by the vice- president Mr. Joseph Mather, treasurer, Mr. James Parr, trustees Messrs. Peter Edgar, William Eckersley, James Grealy, auditor, Mr. Joseph Adams,general secre- tary, Mr. Laurence Weir and others. The Chairman expressed the pleasure it gave him to have the privilege of presiding over such a large gather- ing of parents and representatives of children belonging to the Society. The year's working had been very satisfactory from everyipoint of view. The funds had risen to considerably over .£3,000 and the numerical condition was almost as good and he felt sure they would all agree with him that their position that night gave them every reason to be proud. There had been a fair amount of work which the officers and committee had had to deal with in the way of investments, and they were proud to say it was bringing in splendid interest. The new rules were of a praiseworthy charac- ter and commended themselves to every thoughful member. He felt sure they were with him proud of their permanent officers—wan of good, sound ability, in every respect.and in conclusion he trusted the collectors would give the General Secretary all the assistance that lay in their power. Mr. LAWRENCE WErrt. the general secretary, pre- sented his annual report of the past ysar's transactions which showed that the Society's funds now amounted to £3,053 Hs. 2.1 d. compared vith £2,862 Ss. Sd. for the year previous algaint of £l9l. 10s. 6id. The numerical condition was 1421 compared with 1361 last year. The Secretary briefly pointed out the advantages gained by their newly constructed table of benefits and was pleased to say how effectively it was working amongst the section the committee desired it should act. Several claims had been paid the extra allowance for over 10 years membership and in some instances for over 25 years membership. In conclusion he said that with the staff of collectors he had at his hapds he had not the least fear for the success of the Society. The auditors reported that the accounts and all books kept by the general secretary also the treasurer's and collectors' books were examined and found to be very satisfactory. Mr. Joseph Mather was elected president and Mr. Thomas Saskerville vice-president for the your, Mr Peter Edgar being again elected Auditor. The remainder of the evening was spent in discussing the revised rules and the advantages derived from them Which would place the members in a better position in every respect. A vote of thanks Was accorded to the catering presi- Aent Mr. Joseph Ashton for the courteous manner in Which he had coneucted the business during his term of office. Mr. ASHTON in response said it had always been a ~Pleasure to him to attend the meetings of the Society ..arni he sincerely hoped the Society wink) be prosperous.

CHAPTER I.

Zr was shortly after midnight and the late January air was biting in its intensity. Yet the steam of great heat rose from the flanks of a cab-horse, as it trotted down Regent Street. . Obviously it had come far and at no gentle pace. Swish went the cabman's whip, and the tired horse bravely endeavoured tea quicken it flagging pace.

The occupant of the cab seemed equally oblivious of the heat of tha horse and the coldness of the air. He was leaning forward with his elbows on the doors of the hansom) with a pre-occupied air, humming un• musically to himself r!.* -I

Asa matter,of fact, Sir Henry 'l,lornington was unconscious of the fact that he was humming. He was deep in thought with strange, weird ideas thudding ceaselessly through' his brain. His massive ugly face with its broad nose and long upper lip was set firm, and his eyes were staring hard at the splash-hoard. The horse's hoofs and the lilt of the .refrain seemed to fit in together, but his mind was many miles away. Sir Henry .11fornington was one of the best known men, in England, liked by few, but respected by all. From the world of _finance he had migrated to the world of politics, and, rising by sheer force of intellect, he occupied the important post in the Cabinet of Chan- cellor of the Exchequer. His Budgets, follow- ing a time of great national extravagance, were marvels of economy. Each year the income-tax was reduced, and great was his fame throughout the land proportionately enhanced. His photographs were in all the shop windows, hobnobbing familiarly with the leading actresses of the day, and he was regarded'as the chief prop of the Government. Without him it was constantly prophesied they would not remain in a day. He was essentially the strong Man of Lord Fulford's administration.

The cab drew up with a sudden jerk, nearly I dislodging the Cabindt Minister. The shock aroused him for an instant, and lie stepped out and paid the cabinan. A few 'yards behind him another cab drew up with an even more sudden jerk, but the occupant was on. the alert and, putting ,his hand through the trap; issued a fresh order to the cabman.

Withou't looking round Sir Henry Morning- ton entered the great doors of the Carlton Club. It was a full night. London was full for the opening of Parliament, which was meeting early to make amends for the past unprofitable session. _

Continuing to hum his refrain with a pre- occupied air he made his way into the smok- ing-room without a word to anyone. He nodded curtly to one or two acouaintance without seeming to see them. The shortness of his manner, however, occasioned little sur- prise, as outside his small clique of intimates it was habitual, hence his unpopularity. He walked straight up to a table 2nd rang the bell sharply. . _

" 413r-ing me a stiff brandy and soda," he said to the waiter, ceasing to hum for an instant. The conversation in his vicinity stopped for a moment in wonder. It was seldom that the Chancellor of the Exchequer was seen to drink anything, especially spirits. ' He must be thinking of the Budget," laughed a cheery cynic with a rubicund face, and everyone smiled at the suggestion. He swallowed it at a gulp, while the waiter watched him in respectful awe, and then walked across to a writing table in the corner, regardless of public opinion. Sitting down he picked up a pen and dashed off a couple of notes in his large, bold handwriting, character- istic of the man.

"My dear Colgate," ran one, "It strikes me that we are on the r'ye of the greatest sensation of the twentieth century, and funny things will transpire within the next twenty- four hours. I dare not put even a hint on paper, and to-morrow morniag will do just as well, as no harm can be done to-night. My brain, usually pretty cool, is thudding with excitement, and I can hardly think clearly. I am in a great quandary, but I see my duty, and I shall do it without flinching. More I cannot say even to you at the moment. Besides, the matter is one which needs dis- cussion and can only be properly explained face to face. I will be round at your private house to-morrow morning at nine sharp, and am writing Verschoyle to meet us there. Till then, yours sincerely,

- HENRY MORNINGTON." This note he addressed to the " Right Hon- ourable William Colgate," a colleague in the Cabinet and His Majesty's Secretary for Home Affairs.

The other, which was shorter but similar in contents, he addressed t Sir Hugo Vers. choyle, the head of Scotland Yard. sending them to their respective private addresses.

As he pressed •down the envelopes he began to bum the same refrain, more out of tuna than ever.

As he -rose a new I,Tember of the House, more temerous than tactful, went up and greeted hi, asking him some trivial question. The Chancellor had congratulated him earlier in the evening upoh having won his seat frcnn the Opposition, and he was anNious to appear intimate with the big man of the party. Sir Henry hardly seemed to hear him. „ ... •

" To-morrow," he answered absent-mind- edlY ; " to-morrow, ask me again to-morrow." The latest addition to the party moved away crestfallen, wavering already in his allegiance and muttering to himself what sounded like "ill-mannered brute, everyone says so."

A junior colleague addressed him, but, to his surprise and chagrin fared little better. The answer was curt to the point of dis- courtesy, and Sir Henry scarcely appeared to realise to whom he was speaking or what he was saying. _ _ _

Then he rang the bell for another brandy and soda and gulped it as greedily as the first. " It's little short of a scandal," murmured an advanced advocate of temperance aghast. "Shoo," answered his companion, nodding sententiously, "something most have upset old Mornington. He's the most temperate than in the world."

Meanwhile the Minister under discussion stalked sternly out of the smoking-room with. out a single good-night or so much as a nod to anyone.

Cab, sir? " inquired the porter deferen- tially. Sir Henry shook his head. " No, thanks." he said shortly, " I'll walk. I want to think," he murmured to himself, i" I want to think."

And he began humming his interrupted refrain.

" Curse the thing," he muttered angrily to himself. " I can't pet it out of my head. it seems to haunt and taunt me." On the other side of the road, a few yards in his rear, walked a tall lithe man of just over thirty with a clean-shaven face. His mouth, too, was set, and he kept fingering something in his overcoat pocket. He was walking about the same pace as the Cabinet Minister and keeping well in the shadow.

It was nearly one o'clock and the streets were beginning to clear. It was too cold to hang about after closing time, and everyone was hurrying off to bed. The one idea seemed to possess everybody, as the keen frost cut their faces and the wind whirled round the • corners with icy boisterousnesS. Sir Henry Mornington thd not appear aware of the intensity of the cold, but walked on at an easy pace down Pall Mall and up St. James's Street. The wind seemed parti- cularly cold at the .open corner ; and the -other man's teeth began to chatter slightly, as much from the tense state of his nerves as from +he temnerature. as he followed him no the hill—which, with its lamps in perspective and deserted pavements, looked unusually steep

* din not seem to occur to the Li:lances= of the Exchequer that some one might b* following him; and he kept methodically on his chin in his chest. He turned sharp to the left into Piccadilly xossed the road, and went up Berkeley Streel into Berkeley Square. He kept round to the Left into Mount Street, making for Park Lane, where his town house was situated amidst the hybrid financiers of all nations, with whom he was eternally at fiscal war. As he crossed Park Street, the man behind stealthily quickened his pace,, crossing the road with extraordinary agility, and drawing his hand from his overcoat pocket. Just at the corner of Park Lane in the deep shadow, he sprang on his victim before he sould turn. A dull plud, and the weapon went home. The Chancellor of the Exchequer fell forward on his face without a sound beyond that of a falling body. The other man withdrew his weapon with a sharp twist and a jerk; and, bending over his victim, he half-turned him over and fumbled for a minute with his coat. Then, turning round, he hurried up Park Street and across Grosvenor Square, breath ing heavily. But London was asleep; and nothing could be heard but his sharp, short breath and the tinkle of a hansom two streets away. A distant church - clock chimed a quarter- past one with a clear frosty note. • •

Ten minutes •later a policeman noticed a dark object in the shadow between Park Street tad Park Lane.

He crossed the road and b'ent down before he realised it was a man.

"Drunk," he murmured to himself, op- timistically.. .

He flashed his lantern on the prostrate body, and for the first time noticed that his foot was in a dark, coagulated, lalf-frozen puddle. He was almost new to the force, and it was too much for his nerves.

"Murder I Murder I " he roared, stepping back and then he began to blow his whistle like a man possessed. In a moment, which 'seemed to the young constable a long lifetime, he was joined by two others.

"Don't stand there like a gibbering lunatic," said one, who was a sergeant. Y Let's see what's wrong." With the coolness of experience he bent over the body and turned it over, while the ether gave him a light from his lantern. "Dead, In afraid," he said, in a low voice: V Stabbed plumb in the back, and no half job either."

Then even his nerve gave for an instant. and he seemed petrified. " Great God, Jim I" he gasped. Look!"

!' Look I

" What ? " said the other, stolidly, seeing nothing. "Look at his buttonhole I The White Rose That makes the third. There's some devil's work about to-night I " Then the other realised.

"I wonder who it is ? " he said, with horrified eagerness. F‘ The other two were big pots." They stooped anxiously over the corpse and examined the white face with fascinated eyes.

Simultaneously they recognised the well- known features.

"Sir Henry Mornington " said the ser- geant, in a low voice husky with horror. " 'Swelp me I" groaned the other. f` The greatest of all I " _ .

THE Home Secretary sat up in bed in response to his valet's call, and rubbed his eyes.

The man,. himself half asleep, but dis- sembling it dutifully, placed his chocolate and his letters by the bedside, with a pile of morning papers beyond. " Eight o'clock, sir," said the man, as he drew up the blinds. •

" Thank you, Charles," answered his master, looking obliquely at the dull grey of the winter morning, and shivering slightly. "Give me my warm jacket." Then, propped in bed with three pillows, he picked up his letters and began to run through them. This was Mr. Colgate's morning formula: He always lived by rule, but more especially In the morning before his wits had had time to collect themselves. _ _ . _ _

He always looked through his letters before rising, not so much the insides as the enve- lopes, sorting out the wheat of private life from the chaff of begging petitions and busi- ness correspondence. Out of the pile-on his knees he threw some back on to the table, keeping the others beside him on the bed.

This done, he sipped his chocolate and began to open them. Take those downstairs and put them on the library table," was his next invariable order; and Charles disappeared Then he read his private correspondence with the slow appreciation of the man who expects pleasant things. At last he came to the Chancellor of the Exchequer's letter, and recognised the big, bold writing at once. "I wonder what Mornington wants ? " he murmured, reflectively. He read the letter once, then again slowly, and then a third time; and a puzzled look same over his face.

"It sounds extraordinary! " he muttered —" most extraordinary I The greatest sen- sation of the twentieth century." Dare not put even a hint on paper.' 'Verschoyle to meet us.' Dear, dear! it is extraordinary 1 But I must get up if they are to be here at nine. And unless—unless poor old Morning- ton is going off his head—he has been over- working lately —it looks as though there's something important in the air." He emptied his cup of cocoa and jumped out of bed—an active, keen man of fifty, with piercing black eyes, and hardly a grey hair on his head. Acumen, tempered with good humour, was shown on his face; and there were few, if any, more popular men in the Cabinet.

" Twenty - past eight," he commented to himself, as he walked towards his dressing- room. "1 must hurry up." At that moment the front-door knocker re- echoed violently through the house, and then again, followed by an unmerciful tug at the bell.

"I'll have those telegraph boys scalped," reflected the Home Secretary, grimly. " But I wonder who is wiring at this unearthly hour ? " The house seemed aIl of a sudden in a tur- moil, and feet could be heard running hithet and thither. A moment later he heard voices outside the dressing-room door. " I must see your master at once! " said a firm, though excited, voice, which the Home Secretary seemed to recognise. "The matter does not permit of a second's delay." Mr. Colgate opened the door himself to face the intruder. . .

" Verschoyle ! " he exclaimed, in' amaze- ment, taken aback by the unexpectedness of his appearance. " Yes," responded the head of Scotland Yard. " Can you spare me a moment? It Ls very prgent." The man's manner and his extraordinary intrusion convinced the other that no time was to be lost.

" Go into my sitting-room on the lett," he said, motioning Charles to lead the way ; " and I'll put on a dressing-gowr and join you." • A second later they were alone. It was a strange victnre in the dell light of the winter morning—the Cabinet Minister in a dressing. gown, and the celebrated detective unshaven, and, for ol.ce. shaken out of his .wonted im- perturbability. "He little thought, when he Hampstead, a woman of great character and no small intellect. H i Her nfluence upon him i wrote, how he was going to supply l the was n most matters paramount, and she greatest sensation of the century.' " alone could check his exuberance or stir his indolence. She was essentially a leader and would brook no interference. Thus the Duke and the Duchess suited each other admirably, and through all their married life had pre- served for each other ' a warm feeling of regard—he looking upon her as one who would aid his enthusiasm or equally readily take all responsibilities off his shoulders, while she recognised in him almost the only man who by birth and wealth could have given her her proper position in the great world she loved to rule. His weaknesses made him dependent upon her, and gave her oppor- tunities of exerting and asserting herself;! and for this reason he was dearer to her for his weak points than another man would have been for his strength. The principal characteristic of the Duchese of Hampstead was ambition,—an inordinate ambition which nothing could satisfy. In society she was supreme, and in the eyes of the country she ranked next to the Royal , Family, with whom she was on terms of unusual intimacy. But the fact remained that there, and there alone, she had to take second place; and, therefore, she was jealous, silently but passionately jealous of royalty. This she would have confessed to no one, and did not even acknowledge to herself. But she had never forgiven the un- enthusiastic reception of a hint from her of the desirability of a union between her only son, Lord Belsize, and an eligible royal princess. The Duke of Hampstead drew a large share of his huge income from property in London ; and in town he possessed two great mansions. One of these stood at a corner of Belgrave Square, and was one of the historic houses of London. The other, situated on his Hampstead property, from which he took his title, was a fine Old Elizabethan pile, ' standing right on the Heath in its own grounds, and surrounded by a high wall. In the country, in addition to a castle in Scotland, the Duke was seated at Southsea Castle, a splendid property on the Hamp- shire coast, ca the confines of the New Forest, and overlooking the Solent. 1 At the age of forty-six the Duchess of Hampstead was still a most beauliful woman. so well preserved that she appeared fully ten years younger. She was tall, with a , very handsome figure; but perhaps the most ' noticeable, point about her was the stateli- ness of her carriage. When she entered a , room, there was an instinctive hush, as ' people watched her sweep in slowly and ' greeefullv. She was the mother of two children, a boy and a girl. The former, Viscount Belsize, was four-and-twenty years of age„ and the greatest parti in London. He was, in addition to his more tangible endowments, smart and handsome, and held a commission in the loth Hussars. The latter, the Lady Sabina Belsize, was just twenty, and wal counted a great beauty. She was neither so tall nor so stately as her mother, but what she lost in these respects she gained in a certain charm. which made her more acces

CHAPTER 111.

The whole of Great Britain was in a turmoil over the murder of the Chancellor of the Exchequer. The sensation in London itself was. unparalleled. . . .

The 'morning papers contained the barest details, which had been received just as they were going to press, combined with the stock biographies of the late politician. But the evening papers were almost in hysterics. They were full of rumours and theories, possible and impossible; and the sub-editors were kept busy bringing out edition after edition, which sold almost before they could begot down west.

London turned out in force; and Park Lane, near the fatal spot, had to be guarded by a strong cordon of police. His home, in which the dead Minister lay, was not held sacred by the excited mob; and there, too, a large force of police had to be l'stationed. The' morning was one long procession of carriages and telegraph boys bringing con- dolences.

Business seemed almost in a state of paralysis in the excitement of the hour; and even society looked solemn, with eyes of fear. The blow that had struck down the Chancellor of the Exchequer seemed to have struck chill fear into many hearts. He was the third ; who would be the next ? That was the unspoken question in the minds of every public man and all connected with them.

The article in the Evening Coma was typical of the rest. It was edged with mourning, and above it appeared large headlines:

MURDER OF THE CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER. . APPALLING cßunt. THE " WHITE ROSE" MYSTERY. THE THIRD OF THa SERIES.

"In the early hours of the morning," it ran, "as nearly as can be ganged, between one o'clock and ; a quarter past, Sir Henry MOrnington,. the Chancellor of the Exchequer and the greatest fiscal Minister since the days of Mr. Gladstone, was found murdered at the corner of Park Lane, not a hunched yards from his own front door. There has been no such srnsation since Ballingham shot Mr. Percival, the Premier, in the lobby of the House of Commons on May rah, 1812, a century ago. But even that occurrence did not create the stir of the present tragedy. In those days papers were few and comparatively little read, and news took time to spread through the country. Again, Sir Henry Mornington has long been acknowledged the grea test living politirinn and the man who sways not only the, destiny of the Cabinet, but also the destiny./ of the country. Further, and above all, this murder appears not to be an isolated act of vengeance or lunacy, but the third of an organised series, "Last December it will be remembered (the horror is still fresh in everybody's mind) that Viscount Poole, t& Under-:•;iecretary fot Von:47n Affairs. Nati Wild on the night of thc

CHAPTER IV.

tibia, and, therefore, more lovable. Her eyeS and hair were very dark brown, and her complexion clear and brilliant. Her mouth was firm without being forbidding, while her small nose lent a dainty touch of femininity to the whole. In character, she combined her mother's force of will with her father's impetuosity, which rendered her a rather remarkable study, and led her at times into doing rash things, regardless of convention. Though for two seasons she had been out, she was still unmarried. This, however, was not for want of being asked; and it was ,part of the social curriculum of those who dared to be refuasd at some time or another by Lady " Bina " Belsize, as she was always called behind her back, and to her face by the few intimates who had the right. Still her mother evinced no anxiety, and it was rumoured that she had her own views on the subject.

On a bleak January morning the Duchess of Hampstead was seated at the bureau in her boudoir in Belgrave Square, attending to her private correspondence. She did not. however, appear as diligent as usual, and she was idly scribbling on the blotting paper, deep in thought. . „

"'Where vAll it all end ? " she murmured. "Once started there is no turning back; but I never realised what it would lead to; and," she added, "for once I never properly counted the cost." It was not often that she owned herself even so far in the wrong. Her meditations were interrupted by the somewhat abrupt entrance of the Duke. For three months past he had been in one of his moods of intense activity and enthu- siasm, and he was . hardly ever still. She knew the phase so well, and always wondered how long it would last before it yielded to his natural plethoric inertia. In his hand he held a batch of morning papers. _ _

I` Of course you've seen the papers ? " he asked, taking up his stand on the hearth rug. The Duchess nodded, twiddling her pen meditatively. " Mornington's done for," went on the Duke excitedly ; "and it seems a pretty clean job. No trace or clue left behind. The Duchess nodded again. "He was a great man in many ways," she said slowly. "Yes," responded the duke," but obstinate as a bullock. No One could make him budge an inch after he had . ottce spoken. It's a pity, a great pity," he concluded, as though conceding a point a trifle grudgingly; "but I suppose it can't be helped now." . i "Let us change the subject, James," in- terrupted the duchess, with a touch of im- patience in her voice. "I'm tired of horrors. We've lived on nothing else for weeks."

There was a momentary silence. Then she went on more quietly: "Have you heard that Sabina has refused Lord Branksea ? "

" No," rejoined the duke, with some interest and energy ; "when was that ? " "Yesterday afternoon; she only told me this morning. "Humph." commented her husband, pul- ling his moustache inconsiderately hard, "I thought he looked a bit black last night. I only hope he won't cut up crusty, that's all. And old Swanage too. It would be deuced awkward just now."

The duchess acquiesced again with a nod. •

"But it would. never hive done at all, James; and you know it. We have other views for tho child, and though Branksea may be a big partt in many ways, he is a very poor matc t compared with the other." "I know, I know," said the duke, almost fretfully, "but it's deuced awkward, as I said ; and I wish it hadn't occurred."

"Well it can't be helped now," answered the duchess decisively ; "we must make the best of it. Bina has a big destiny before her, and neither Branksea nor anyone else is going to interfere with it, if I have a word to say in the matter."

"Of course you will, my dear," inter- c.cted his Grace grimly; "you always have, including the last word too But what about this big ball of yours ? "

"Everything is completely ready and all my arrangements are made. The usual people are doing the flowers and Gudgeon assures me that everything is in the house, down to the last truffle, and half the things cooked. The King and Queen arrive at ten- thirty sharp: and the ball opens with a royal quadrille. After that there will be bridge—you'll have to play, James, whether you want to or not; and the younger people can dasice and flirt and do what they like." "All right, my dear," said the duke wearily, relapsing into his old inertia at the prospect of a function. At that moment the door was opened with unusual unceremoniousness and Lord Belsize burst in, followed closely by his sister. "I say, guv'nor," he began, without pre- face, " I say, mater, isn't this horrible—poor old Morny ? It has given Bina and me quite a beastly turn. They'll be sticking the guv'nor next with their devilish tricks." "Shish, Charlie," said the duchess, who had for an instant grown pale at the sudden interruption ; "don't talk like that. It is very terrible about Sir Henry Mornington ; and your father and I have just been dis- cussing the matter." "What do you make of it, guv'nor ? " asked the irrepressible young hussar, looking over towards his father with an affectionate familiarity. " Bina and I think it's a case of anarchists—the devils ought all to be strung up, only that's too good for them. What do you think of the ' white rose' part of it ? " " I don't know, Charlie, I'm sure : I can't say," answered his father, with a grave anxious look on his face. "But I don't think it has much to do with it, do you, my dear ? " he concluded, turning to the duchess. "I'm sure I can't say," answered the duchess, with decision and some asperity in her voice.

" I think it must be a symbol, a token of some sort." said Bina in a low moice, speaking softly.

"What a fanciful child you are," said the duke, patting her head gently. His daughter was his greatest weak point, and the only thing over which he could sustain a perma- nent enthusiasm. "But what about the ball to - night ? " he continued, changing the subject. "Oh. it ought to be lovely," answered Bina enthusiastically " and I hear that lots of people are awfully indignant with mother for not _having. asked_ them.' _ _ .

The Duchess of Hampstead smiled grimly. " I say, mater," broke in Lord Belsize, " I tell you a chap you might send a card to— the Earl of Ben Nevis. I was only introduced to him yesterday at the club, and took a great fancy to him. He seems very popular, and has only just returned from abroad after a long spell. First he went out to the Boer War with his regiment, and stayed there in the police for several years after it was over. Then he went travelling in all sorts of un- explored parts, discovering things and shooting big game. He only got back two days a.go after all these years, and is quite the latest lion in a small way. He's a major, a V.C., a D. 5.0., and all sorts of other things." . . .

Belsize spoke with all a younger man's en- thusiasm for an older man who has made a name for . himself.

The duchess listened thoughtfully, but a light came into the duke's dreamy eyes. . .. . _

Yes, my dear," he said, " why not send young Ben Nevis a card ? ".

Then, leaning over her shoulder, he added in a low voice : "He may become one of us; he would be very useful."

THE EXAMINER.

AUGUST 19111 1905.

It looks as if the Peace TELE PEACE Conference at the

NEGOTIATIONS. American

Portsmouth

would fail. Japan has demanded, as everybody knew she would demand, the cession of Saghalien, and a " reimbursement " for her expenses in the war. " Reimburse- 1 ment " is an admirably diplomatic term. it means indemnity, but sounds like something else. We are told that Russia will pay neither in money nor in territory, as she considers such demands injurious to her dignity. She offers Korea to Japan, and is willing to evacuate Manchuria, which evacuation, by the way was promised several times before the war. When Russia makes a pledge, violates it, and then fights an unsuccessful campaign, she seems to think that 1 the fulfilment of her pledge, brought about by the fortune of war, is an ample atonement to her victorious adversary. The Japanese do not think so, nor does anybody else. Russia is beaten in this war, and she cannot evade the penalty she has brought. upon herself. If she I had been victor she would have exacted an indemnity from Japan, and would not have called it " reimbursement." But now the boot is on the other leg she imagines that she can defy both precendent and common sense. 31. Wirrn must have known quite well before he left St. Petersburg that Japan would insist on the formal retrocession of Saghalien, which was originally her property. He must have known that she would demand an indemnity. Was he prepared to accede to their demands after a vigorous protest, or was he instructed by the CZAR to break off the negotiations when they touched a vital point for Russian hoinoer ? The situation is obscure. It hardly seemed worth M. WITTE'S while to go to America if he were not empowered to accept the terms he knew to be inevitable. But he may have thought that a dramatic rejection of those terms would make a favourable impression in America, where Russia is anxious to revive her good name. The ingenious persons who are attached to the staff of M. WITTE'S mission are busily circulating remarkable state- ments about the war. It has not gone very badly for Russia after all. The Japanese successes are trifling ; moreover, they have been greatly exag- gerated at Tokio. Look at the time it has taken OYAMA. to reach his present position. The Russians have only to remain on the de- fensive and Japan will be helpless. Her finances are in a very bad state. She has borrowed all she can, and very soon she will be exhausted. It is true that Saghalien is in her hands at present, and that Russia cannot recover it until she has another navy. But that will all come in good time. Meanwhile, there is not the smallest reason why she should agree to the cession of the island, Such are the argu- ments put forward by M. WITTE'S secretaries and newspaper sympathisers. They have not had much effect upon the world in general. Few people believe that Russia is in a condition to carry on the war indefinitely. There is small prospect of its ceasing to be a bureaucratic war and becoming a national war. The hearts of the Russian people have never been in it; why should they embrace it now ? The financial credit of Russia is much worse than that of Japan. The Alikado's Govern- ment can borrow all the money it wants, and the Mikados troops can seize an immense tract of Russian territory on the Amur, to say nothing of Vladivostock. The assumption that a waiting game would pay Russia better than acquiescence in Japn's peace proposals is an extremely hazard- ous speculation.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
A Strike Averted.

We are pleased to learn that the representa- tives of employers and employed in the cotton trade have arrived at an amicable settlement of their dispute. Strikes, at best, occasion much unhappiness and it is with feelings of satisfaction that, in this case at any rate, a strike has been averted and that a conciliatory attitude prevails between the masters and men. A gratifying feature of the protracted conference at Manchester has been the splendid spirit shown by both parties to the dispute. Neither can claim a victory, which is perhaps as well, though the operatives are to ba congratulated en obtaining a bonus for three I months. While not equal to a 5 per cent. advance, the gain is considerable and fully merited by the improved trade conditions. The "Trade" and the Council._

_ - We hear there is a strong probability of at least four publicans entering the lists for municipal honours next November. The licensed victuallers are much concerned about the operations of the Compensation Act, and are anxious to strengthen their position by increased repro- sentatim on the Town Council, though that

authority does not, of course, constitute the Compensation Committee, which is composed entirely of magistrates. It will be a matter to be greatly deplored if the brewing and licensing interest on the Town Council is strengthened. The malign influence of "the trade" on local affairs has long been a bye-word in Warrington, and just now the Council is freer from the brewer and the publican than we ever remember it to have been. There are only three gentlemen in the Council including the Mayor, who are connected with the drink traffic—a numerical proportion which is ada- quate and perhaps not excessive. Without any antipathy to the publicans es individuals—most of them are estimable men—we should be very sorry to see them striving for a stronger hand in the management of the town's affairs, and it will be I the bounden duty of the temperance societies and such progressive bodies as the Social Ssrvice !League, Municipal Reform Association, and Free I Church Council to strenuously oppose any attempt on their part to bolster up trade interest. 1 The Walton Show.

For once the members of the Warrington and District Agricultural Society have been favoured with a really fine day for their annual show. As a result the attendmce at Walton on Thursday was probably a record, for thousands of Warring- tonians who would not lay claim to any special knowledge of agriculture, were only to) pleased to spend the afternoon amid the delightful surround- ings of Sir Gilbert Greenelt's demesne. The show itself fully maintained the high reputation of the society wnich from very small beginnings has risen to the front rank of local exhibitions. As usual the ' splendid display of horses was the great feature and would have done credit to larger shows than War- rington. It was particularly gratifying that the exhibitors more than held their own in the open sections, notwithstanding very keen competition. Successful as was the show,however,the afternoon's pleasure was marred by a serious accident which, for the time being,created consternation. Hitherto the Warrington Show has enjoyed an immunity from such unfortunate occurences, and the affair —which was purely accidental—occasioned very genuine regret.

Next American President. The election of a new President of the United States does not recur until 1908, but our cousins are never slow in these matters, and the topic has been under frauk and apparently cheerful discus- sion for months past. There are three or four prime favourites ; there may be others as time spins its changes. Secretary Taft, Senator Foraker, and Governor Herrick are at present making the running in that order, and they are all Buckees. They all come from Ohio, and Ohio provides the Republic with more men for the top offices than any other State does. The Secretary for War, the " Times" correspondent suggests, is easly first to-day. He was, to all intents and pur- poses Secretary of State when Mr. Hay went abroad for his health,and President Roosevelt was lately advising those who had high State business to transact in his own absence to "see Taft,' so that the phrase has become a colloquialism As Civil Governor, Mr. Taft straightened out the tangle in the Pailippines, three years ago, but though his heart was in that work the great lawyer bowed to President Roosevelt's order to come home and manage the War Office, and it is believed that he would further sacrifice his feelings and, at the bidding of his compatriots, undertake from White House the management of the Re- public. It is a tribute to American versatility that the War Secretary is said to have ambitions run- ning in the direction of the Chief Justice of the Supreme Court of the United States. Mr. Roo should have an equally good chance if it were a question only of quality and not of State senti- ment.

A Neglected Possession. The things which are nearest our hands are the most neglected. Tnese are days when most things are turned to money, if it be at all possible ; yet,as a correspondent of a contemporary points out, English people habitually ignore the profit-pro- ducing possibilities of one of Nature's gifts to them. All through the country this month great crops of blackberries are ripe and ready for use. Com- paratively little of the fruit—in proportion to the immense quantities that come to maturity—is picked or eaten. Yet it is not that English people do not like blackberries. There is probably no fruit which is more generally in favour, and in the London market it is readily sold. But the black- berries which reach the Londoner are not the fruit of the country districts of England. They come from Brittany, where the rural population, spurred on, of course, by the enterprise of shippers, engage regularly, and with much pecuniary success, in the work of harvesting the wild fruit. At best it is a poor article that gets to London, Iwith all the juice squeezed out of it and a Iwooden flavour; but as no better can be obtained I owing to the lack of enterprise at home, it has, ' perforce, to be put up with. What the trade means to Brittany may be gathered irom the fact that the amount realised from it last year was over £B,OOO the wholesale merchants in this coantry paying at the rate of £lO or £l2 per ton for the fruit. It seems a pity that we should go lacross the Channel for fruit which we could get in better condition if there were only some organised Ilsystem of collection among the inhabitants of I rural districts.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
Is Scotland on the Down Grade?

This question is asked in a—ct-i-. 1;;;; 'number of a leading -eview. It suggests Dr. Johnson's famous joke about a Scotchn3an's best prospect. It reminds one of astonishing figures given awhile ago es to the proportion of responsible positions held by Scots in England—bank manager- ships, insurance managerships, actuarial and other positions requiring at once responsi- bility and sagacity. It was a surprise to most people. Clannishness would not explain it. Men have to be fit to hold these places, and it is not enough to say that there are others equally fit who were not called. " Malagrowther's " observa- tions in the " National Review " are quite of the serious order. He complains that Scottish litera- ' tura is a thing of the past. The publications, he says, are nearly all "educational " or informative ; they have no inspiration about them. Since the death of Burns " Scotland," he adds, " has pro- duced only two poets, and these second raters, James Hogg and Alexander Smith." It is only right that credit should be given to the quality of Scotch journalism, and that it should be remem- bered also that some Englishmen should have been allowed to participate in it. Comparison may safely be made with anything else of the kind that is done in the country, and though the imagination seems to have dulled as regards belles lettres and art, Scotland has still raised within its own borders magnificient 'milers. In the South African War they did splendidly, and reminded us that Scot- land has greater qualities than those of mere money-getting. It has got money, nevertheless, and the writer is correct in saying, so far as statis- tics will guide, that the country is now better oft than ever. Even its golf courses have helped.
