WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON.

WEEK ENDING AUGUST 12th, 1905.

During the week, the following births and deaths were registered:—Warrington Registration District births, 37, deaths, 32. The excess of births over deaths was 5. The birth-rate was equal to 26.3. The death-rate from all causes was equal to 24.0 per 1,000 against 17.2 the average of the prevte us 4 weeks and against 21.3 the corresponding period of last year. The death rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 6.0 per 1,000, against 4.1 the average rate of the previous 4 weeks and against 9•9 the corresponding period of 1904. The ages of mortality were:- 04 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65—up. Total 12 3 1 10 6 32.

The deaths included-1 from measles, C from diarrhoea, 2 from enteritis, 3 from phthisis, 1 from other tubercular diseases, G from lung diseases, 13 from all other causes. The following eases of infectious diseases have been reported during the week :—Scarlet fever, 1 case. Total number of patients in hospital, week ending 12th August, 1905, 22. J. GUEST GORNALL, Medical Officer of Health.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Conscription will never be accepted by the people. of England.—ltev. Thomas Cole in the "Fortnightly. * * * We are glad to hear that there is a continued ini- provement in the health of Mr. Sam Woods, president of the Lancashire County Miners' Federation.

hill r

• * * "An odd thing that I have noticed when the average Englishman or Englishwoman speaks French is that they seem ashamed to give it anything at all re- sembling the true accent. I believe they think it sounds affected ! "—" Truth." * * * There is a happy hunting ground' at the corner of Liverpool road and Mikup street for the members of our" Beautiful Warrington Societ)." The smell of Gorgonzola at its best—or worst—cannot approach that which permeates the atmosphere at this point. During the trial of a case in the City of London Court it transpired that tinned fruit exported from abroad is often stored in public warehouses in Londoa for as long as 10 years, and that the contents are per- fectly good at the end of that time, being sold on the London markets at highly remunerative prices. Such longevity will put even the railway sandwich to shame! * * And now, in these later days, the Church ba4 fallen into almost obscurity as a power in the moral and civic life of the nations. Its form remains, its habiliments are still gorgeous, but it walks behind, not in front of the State, and its gestures and speecLi are almost unheeded in the great march of the nation. "'Labour Leader."

* * * Is the British race dying out? The startling intelligence is contained in the quarterly return of the Registrar-General that the birth-rate for the second quarter of 1905 "is the lowest birth-rate recorded in any second quarter since civil registration was estab- lished." The actual figures were equivalent to 27.3 per 1,000 of the population, the mean rate in ten pre- ceding second quarters being 204. * * * _ _

"I never give a penny to missions or hospitals. A 9 for missions, when I see a professedly Christian army showing the same high level of sobriety and morality shown by the Japanese, and the average British workingman as sober, industrious, and peaceful as the average Chinese or Hindu, it will be time to think of them. The need of medical aid is what we all experi- ence more or less, snd can be insured against."—Mr. Henry Wilson in the "Times."

*s* Mr. Philip Snowden, writing in the current number of the " Independent," is pleased to state that " Lb° work of promoting reform by legislation has passed- away from the Liberal Party." Of course, we can readily understand that there is only one party which is" promoting reform "—the party to which one belongs. We are pleased to learn, however, that the Liberal Party is credited with some reforms in the' past. That, at any rate, is an admission which most speakers on the I.L.P. platform will not grant. * * * Few people, perhaps, have any adequate conception of the amount of correspondence which passes through the post in the course of a year. in 1890 each inhabitant of the United Kingdom (including infants) was represented by a delivery of 45 letters. In 1001 and in each of the two previous years, the deliveries per head had risen to 61. The Briton of to-day i 5 indeed a ready writer :—Newspapers, book packets, circulars, and samples have multiplied in the 15 years from 6371 millions to 1,022 1-10 millions.

* * * The wail of the brewers is very heartrending, 0.131 we can sympathise with them in their business losses. It is very gratifying, however, to hear on their authority that there is a marked decrease in beer- drinking among the working-classes. This should, of course, result in the spread of temperance .0 sobriety. The evidence in this connection is, how" ever, not so strong, and until we see temperance' societies booming, and a considerable fall in tin national drink bill, we shall be sceptical of any re3l change for the better in the habits of the people. The outlook is, nevertheless, distinctly bright for temper" • ance workers.

* * * An amusing incident occurred at the Warrington. County Court on Thursday. A witness had been called into the box and the usher was proceeding to administer the oath when it was found that the testa- ment was missing. X hasty search proved fraitlesg and a lady who had recently completed her testimony was appealed to as to the whereabouts of the missing book. At first she appeared to be in doubt as to tvlint she had done with it when to her great surprise she dis- covered it on her knee. It was promptly restored and the business of the court was resumed.

The Examiner, Saturday, August 19, 1905.

Appointment of School Attendance Officer. Mr. George A. Donaldson, 81, Laira-street, War- rington has been appointed assistant school attend- ance officer under the Warrington Education Committee, to fill the vacancy caused by the death of Mr. Scarisbrick. About 100 applications were received or the post.

The Investigation Committee. The committee appointed to investigate certain charges against members of the Corporation has con- cluded its sittings. The evidence is regarded as of such importance that counsel's opinion is to be taken as to any further steps that may be considered necessary.

Warrington Bridge. Vice-Admiral Sir George Nares, Acting Conservator of the Mersey, has consented to receive a deputation from the Town Council to ask him to reconsider his refusal to sanction the widening of Warrington Bridge by cantilevers. The meeting will take place in Liver- pool next week.

Husband's Tragic Discoirery. About ten o'clock on Monday night, on returning from a week's holiday he had been spending at Worcester, Mr. Herod, an ironmonger, of 69, Cross- lane, Earlestown, was unable, to obtain admittance to his house. Eventually he forced an entrance through a window at the rear of the house, and immediately detected a strong smell of gas. He traced the escape to a study, where he was horrified to find his wife lying on a cone' quite dead. The unfortunate woman, who was about thirty-five years of age, had telegraphed to her husband on Monday, asking him to return at once as she was unwell.

Death of Mr. John Critchley. I We record with regret the death of Mr. John Critchley, of Brightside, Fentahead, which occurred in his 04th year. Mr. Critchley, who was a well- known and highly respected Warringtonian had been a sufferer for a considerable time from paralysis, and his illness took an acute turn on Wednesday, death supervening on Sunday. Many years ago he was fore- man glass cutter for Messrs. Robinson and Bolton, and also carried on business in town as a baker and provision dealer. He was actively associated with the Brick-street Sunday School, acting as superintend- ent and had been connected with the Friars-green Independent Methodist Chapel in all its various offices. He was also connected with the British ' Workman Restaurant Company, and was one of the managers of the Warrington Permanent Benefit Building Society. He took no public part in politics, ' but held Liberal views. He leaves a widow and a son and daughter. The funeral took place on Wednesday 'afternoon at the Warrington Cemetery the service being conducted by Mr. W. Brough, assisted by Mr. W. Wright. The principal mourners were Messrs. Thomas and James Critchley (brothers), J. Gerrard (brother-in-law), William and James, E. and. S Critchley, and W. Woods (nephews), Masters John and William Malcom Barlow (grandsons). Amongst. others present were Messrs. T. Sudlow, S. Dale, J. Gandy, J. Mason, J. Hudson, Ezard, J. Birch, G Monnfield, W. Mounfield, J. hlonnfleld, Chambers, C. Jackson Holmes and W. Roberts, representing the British Workman Company, W. Mercer and Dr. Bennett. The Warrington Workingmen's Permanent Benefit Building Society was also represented. Many beautiful floral tributes were placed on the grave.

LATEST NEWS.

The Cotton Crisis.

---- Strike Averted.---Concession to Operatives.

Five Per Cent. Bonus.

A CONCILIATION SCEIEME SUGGESTED.

After a conference extending over two days and last ing nearly twenty hours, the representatives of employers and employed in the cotton trade on Thursday night arrived at a peaceful settlement, the terms of which are appended. The agreement, athich has been signed by the secre- tary of each organisation represented, is as follows :- 1. That the federation withdraw its application for a 5 per cent, reduction in the Bolton area. 2. That the operatives withdraw their applica- tion for a 5 per cent. increase in wages in the federation area exclusive of Bolton. 3. That the federation of operatives agrees to 5 per cent, bonus being paid for twelve weeks from the first pay day in September, to be paid monthly at all federation mills, exclusive of the Bolton

area. 4. At the end of the twelve weeks the bonus shall lapse and the present rate of wages 81:1411 be paid until the Ist of March, 1906, after which date either side shall be at liberty to take whatever action shall be deemed de.irable with regard to wages advance or reduction. 5. That the joint committees meet together as early as possible for the purpose of framing some scheme for the automatic regulation of wages in future.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
CORN LAW MEMORIES.

---- Things We Mast Not Forget.

In "Corn Law Memories" Mr. J. K. Snowden con- tributes to the Contemporary a gruesome story of the bad times in England previous to Corn Law repeal. Thrifty, religious, excellent people were reduced to almost incredible privations. Mr. Snowden's grand- father ran a weaving shed at Cowling, a Yorkshire village on the Lancashire border. There came a time when my grandmother had nothing to give her children. . . • Being a devout man, my grandfather made them all kneel down and prayed for food, and next morning there was a sack of potatoes in the cellar, lie accepted it with pious grati- tude, asking no questions; but I happen to know that it was stolen for him by a man who was himself "clamming." People were glad to make a meal of nettles,. of stolen turnips, of nuts, of hips and haws, An 'Lush doctor , who went through the manufacturing districts describes the people's food as" oatmeal and water for breakfast, flour and water with a little skimmed milk for dinner, oatmeal and water again for a third meal." In one place a young man who took some potatoes from a huckster's shop was followed to his home. They found an aged mother and two sisters dependent on him. There was a pot upon the fire. On the shop- keeper asking the mother if she knew where her son had got the potatoes, she replied, No ; I was afraid to Ask.' He went to the pot and found a portion of a dead dog, which they were boiling to eat with the potatoes. Mr. Snowden winds up his recital with the remark that the Select Committee 011810 published a body of evidence which showed there had not been one really prosperous year since the Corn Law of 1815 was adopted. This is a page of history which the English people just now can by no means afford to forget.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
GLASGOW'S TRAMWAY PROFITS.

The report of the Glasgo-v Corporation Tramways Committee shows that in the twelve months ended May 31 last the total income from the tramways amounted to £764,790; while the working expenses, excluding depreciation, were £387,167; leaving a balance of £377,623 to be carried to the revenue account. In other words the tramway system of Glasgow is being conducted at something like 50 per cent. Of expenses to receipts. Compared with the previous twelve months this year's gross balance shows an increase of £10,352.

METEOROLOGY.—The mean temperature was Cl Fah and therefore 2 deg. lower than the preceding week The maximum temperature G9F., was recorded on the 7th inst., and the minimum temperature 40F., on the 12th inst.

WARRINGTON SHOW.

BEAUTIFUL WEATHER AND A RECORD EXHIBITION AT WALTON. Serious Accident to a Stud Groom.

Perfect weather, a record entry, and. a splendid attendance of the general public combined to make the Warrington ard District Agricultural Society's sixteenth annual show which was held in Walton Mirk on Thursday the most successful yet organized. The promoters had every reason to congratulate themselves upon being thus favoured. From early in the morn- ing until well on in the day there was brilliant sun- shine while a pleasant breeze tempered the heat. it was only at the close of the show when most of the spectators had started for home that a smart shower was experienced. The Society is indeed fortunate in having the .picturesquely situated grounds at Walton placed at its disposal each year, and the president, Sir Gilbert Greentill, Bart, earns the cordial thanks of all well-wishers of the show by making this concession. A prettier and more suitable place for the exhibition it would not be possible to find within such easy access of the town. Bordered by luxurious foliage,the park corn- mands a very pleasing view of Cheshire country which dll Thursday was looking at its best. A great many people who could hardly be said to be interested in . agriculture availed themselves of the opportunity of spending a few hours amid such delightful surround- ings and breathing the pure country air. As usual , Sir Gilbert threw upon his beautifully-kept gardens and the roomy stables and piggeries were also I inspected with interest. During the day five thousand people from Warrington and aistries , visited the show and the usually peaceful village of Walton presented quite an animated scene. There was an almost continuous stream of vehicles, special omnibuses being run from Warrington. As to the , show itself, it more than maintained the fine reputa- tion which the Warrington Society has already estab- lished. The entries numbered 45S as compared with 416 last year arid constituted a record. While there , was a slight decrease in the munly,r of shire horses shown, it was more than compensated for by an increase in the bunter, hackney, and harness classes. In the cattle section the entries were exactly the same as last year, but a slight increase was noticed in the pig classes. There was also an advance in the exhibits of cheese, but the butter classes were extremely badly supported, only seven competitors entering. War- rington show has long been noted for US excellent dis- play of horses, and this was again the outstanding : feature. Some of the finest animals in the country were to be seen, and exceptionally keen competition in several classes made the task of the judges by no means easy. Mr. Jamesl Gould, of Lyann, was again a foremost exhibitor and scored a series of brilliant successes. He carried off the district champion cup value £lO presented by Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., a special prize value \ g,5, given by Mr. F. Stapleton Bretherton, and numerous other awards. The jumping contest aroused great interest and excitement ran high when the com- petitors were leaning the various obstacles. The Victory of Mr. F. V.Granger's " Nomination " was an I exceedingly popular one. Unfortunately a serious accident marred the pleasure of the afternoon. Joseph Hewitt, stud groom in the employ of Mr. Robert Needham, Berton Lodge, Knutsford, ,was . leading a young hunter from the judging ring when the animal became restive. Hewitt had allowed it too much rein and was unable to keep the horse in hand, with the result that he was knocked down and severely kicked about the head. He was carried to the Secretary's tent insensible, and Drs. Bowden, Braide, and Bowers were quickly on the scene. They found that both the unfortunate man's jaws as well as his nose had been broken, and after first aid had been rendered he was conveyed to tie Warrington Infirmary. His eondi- tion remains serious. _ _ _ .

..... The arrangements for the show had been ad mirably carried out, the officials having spared no pains to ensure the success of the event. Special praise is due ,to Mr. H. Beresford Melville who discharged the secretarial duties. The band of the Yorkshire Hussars was in attendance and, under the conductorship of Mr. S. Cramer-Suckley, submitted a choice programme. Their excellent playing called forth the highest encemiums which frequently found expression in hearty applause. It was the general opinion that the music was one of the most enjoyable features of the day and the band cannot be too highly complimented upon its performances.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
FEATURES OF THE SHOW.

As is always the case, the shire horses came first and foremost among the many interesting features of the show. No better exhibits have been seen at Walton and the tVarrington show has a due reputation for its shires. Mr. James Gould, of Lynam, NNW the most prominent exhibitorAin the district classes "Arley Metal " owned by Mr. James Gould which won the first prize in the class for brood mare four years old with foal nt foot is the mother of the noted stallion " Lymm Lion." She is of splendid bone and feather and has won every time she has been exhibited. The only occa- sion on which she has been shown this year was at Birkenhead, when she carried off the 50 guinea cup. Mr. Gould's " Lyram Lass " which came second in the same class, has for eleven years in succession always won a prize. Mr. Gould also won a first prize with his "Lymm Tom," a splendid foal which was reserve for the cup. Mr. Samuel G. Gould won the first prize with a brown filly foal, which made its first appearance in the show ring. It has fine feather and weight and good movement, and gained practically a walk over. " Cattrall Black Prince," owned by Mr. James Gould, which carried off the first prize in the yearling colt or gelding section, has already gained laurels. In addition to carrying off the third award at the London Shire Show, it secured first at Birkenhead, second at Peter- borough, and first at Liverpool. These are the only occasions on which the animal has been shown this season. In the yearling filly class there was a very close contest between the brown shown by Messrs. H. and R. Ainscough, of Risley, and Mr. Benjamin Wright's (Mobberly) bay, the prize going to Messrs. A inscough. Mr. James Gould gained the premier honours in the two-year-old colt or gelding class with the brown colt " Lymm Spark" which has only made one previous appearance this season, when it gained a first at Prescot in the class for any age. The two-year-old bay "Lymm Blossom " was , a notable winner, carrying off the District Champion Cop presented by Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., the gold 1 medal offered by the Shire Horse Society (London) and the special prize of £5 given by Mr. F. Stapleton IBretherton. She is a good all-round foal with a quiet and excellent movement and has a splendid record of successes, being second at London, first at Derbyshire and West Midland, first at Birkenhead, and third at Liverpool. "Lymm Bloom " the successful :three-year-old filly is another animal of which Mr. Gould has just reason to be proud, having been first at 13.rbyshire and West Midland, first and champion at Birkenhead, and second at the Royal Lancashire show. She is without doubt one of the best foals in the country Mr. Gould scored several excellent successes with shires already mentioned in the open classes. There were some capital exhibits in the gelding classes. There was keen competition in the class for gelding foaled in 1901-1902, between Mr. Edward Davies's (Partington) "Duke" and Mr. 11. E. Gibbon's (Lymm) " Chief " but Mr. Davies's grey was much the bigger animal, with very good legs and feet, and the judges awarded it the first prize. The " Chief " was a very weighty animal, while the third winner (Mr. John Whitlows "Captain") was a very big horse with not much weight. The open class for geldings four years old and upwards included some of the finest animals in England. Mr. A. C: Sparkes' (Altrincham) brown "Oldfield Duke" was the premier winner. Considering its show career this horse has kept nn wonderfully well. It was first at London and a peculiar circumstance is that there, as at the Walton Show, the second award went to Mr. Edward Davies's (Partington) "Invincible." Since the London show, however, "Invincible "has improved considerably and ran its rival very close. Mr. Joseph Park's (Retford) " Blackbird "was a third, being a very good, weighty horse, but not so largi as the other two. Before leaving the shire section, it should be noted that Mr. S. W. Gould failed to enter his foal in the filly foal (district) class or he would almost certainly have won the second prize, as his exhibit had previously beaten the second and third prize-winners in the district class. A notable feature of the shire classes was that the champion, first, second and third reserves were all by Mr. James Gould's " Lymm Lion," while the fourth reserve was "Lymm Lion's dam." The hackneys were a class of unusually high merit, and would have done credit to any local exhibi- tion in the country. Mr. John Makeagne, Golborne Park, was a prominent exhibitor, and his name , figured frequently in the prize list. In the breed- I ing classes, Mr. Makeague secured the premier I award with the thirteen-year-old "Dairy Maid " —a very stylish animal out of old " Ophelirt's " dam Mr. A. C. ApArkss's " Rosemary " is a strong mare of a totally different type, by" Rosacior," while Mr. Good. son's " Polly Ailsa,' the third, is again of another type. There were some promising foals. Very neat and a ell- fashioned is Mr. Makes:7.llo'a bay colt, out of his tidy mare, and sired by " Maze II." " Heathfield Pansy" had quite an easy win in the yearling class. The two or three year old class was headed by Mr. Makeagne's three year old gelding " Gainsboro," probably one of the best geldings of his age, being beautiful in shape and move- ment. Ile was reserve to " Heathfleld Squire" at Liver- pool for the Championship Cup. There was a paddock flit of pony brood mares and of their foals. Harness

classes were splendidly filled, and " Gainshoro "won again, next to him being a smart little pony by" Sir Horace." In the open class under fourteen Miss Clarke's "Berkeley Bobs "won nicely from Mr. T. P. Robinson's " Pinderfield Ruby." and in the class above that height and under fifteen hands Mr. J. S. Sykes's " Fylde Sabrinetta" was a splendid winner. In the larger height class Mr. A. E. Evans's roan won well, but "Silver Ness " ran it very closely for second place. Hunters were a splendid feature. Mr. Robert Need- ham's " Merry maid,"—a useful mare able to breed strong hunters—was first in the breeding classes which are confined to the district. Lady Greenall took second with" Virtue "a good-looking brown, but did not com- pete for a prize, and Mrs. Griffiths's famous old mare Lady Templar " took the money and Sir Gilbert's premium for the best tenant farmer's animal. Third went to Mr. T. P,roady's grey mare. There was a regrettable loaness of entries in the foal section, only two animals being on show. Both the winners are by , " Beckenham." Yearlings varied much, the best being a I bay colt out of Mrs. Geitfiths's old mare, and sired by I "Johnny Morgan." It took the premium also, and the , Hunter Society's medal. The second was a" Methuen " filly, out of the first prize mare. There were some good young hunters in the two or three-year-old class, which was perhaps the best in this section. The saddle classes of hunters were remarkably well filled, and came both from far afield and near home. First there were four- teen equal to carry thirteen stone and not more than fourteen and a half. They were to be novices and not to have won a prize value five pounds. The next class was for horses up to over the top weight of the last and also for novices. Two first-class hunters headed the list. Then came the similar open classes. There was exceedingly keen competition in that for the middle weights the judges having great difficulty in making the awards.

The cattle classes were exceptionally creditable for a local show. The dairy cows were an excellent class, and it is doubtful whether they could be excelled at any local exhibition. There were not many entries of dairy cows in milk, but the quality of the animals was very good. The heifer class on the whole was hardly so good. There was a very useful show of hulls, especi- ally in the younger classes, while the class for bulls born in 1903-4 was exceptionally fine, though Mr. J. R. New- ton's " English Melody" was a very easy first. So keen was the competition in the class for heifers born in 1903 that there was a diversity of opinion between the judges, and a referee had to be called in to decide the awards. 'THE JUMPING CONTEST. To the general public the most interesting feature of the day's proceedings was the jumping contest. As usual a grand stand had been erected, from which a more complete view of the proceedings could be obtained, and it was largely patronized. The ring with its background of beautiful trees is ideally situated, and ' the scene when the spectators had assembled was full of colour and animation. The contest itself was a splendid one, prizes of £2O, £l5, £lO and ,C 5 beiug offered. Eleven competitors put in an appearance, and they included Mr. John Taylor, of Ormskirk, a well- known horseman, who has had the honour of an 'intro- duction to the King. The horses had to leap over several gates, a fence, and a pool of water and, as may readily be imagined, the contest afforded plenty of excitement. One of the riders was thrown, but did not sutler any injury. After an exacting test the judges awarded the first prize to Mr. F. V. Grange, Oak House, Fanadon, whose gelding " Nomination " cleared all obstacles in splendid style. The other winners were :-2nd, —Bromley, The Priory, Shrewsbury; 3rd, John Taylor, Ormskirk ; 4th, G. Leelson, Manor House. Bromboro'.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

A BUSINESSLIKE THIEF.

A peculiar case of theft was heard at Warrington on , Saturday, James Ward,of Rylands's Yard, being charged with stealing 126 lbs of potatoes from a waggon at the Cheshire Lines goods yard and also a wheel barrow from the premises of Messrs. Crompton and Shawcross, con- tractors—The evidence showed that a checker in the employ of the railway company saw the prisoner wheel- ing a barrow containing the potatoes and discovered that he had stolen them from the waggon. Subsequent inquiry revealed the fact that the barrow had been taken irom the yard of Messra.Crompton and Shawcross —Superintendent :Monaghan said that was tho prisoner's 32nd appearance. He was a prowling thief and worked very little—Prisoner was sentenced to four months imprisonment. CRUELTY TO HORSES. At Warrington, on Monday, Patrick Hunt, cabdriver in the employ of Mr. James Smith, cab proprietor, Ormskirk-road, Pemberton, near ‘Vigan, was charged with cruelty to horses by working them in an unfit state.—Smith was charged with causing the same to be worked.—Sergeant Barnett stated that on Bank Holiday he saw Hunt driving three horses attached to a waggonette laden with passengers. He noticed the horses were working rather badly, and on examination found one of them had ten old wounds on the shoulders. Another had a large wound on the back which was raw and bleeding, and the hiad leg was swollen and the home lame.—Another driver,named Ludbrook,was charged with working a pair of horses whilst in an unfit state, and Smith was also charged with causing them to be worked. The horses were attached to a waggonette, and were suiTering from wounds on the breast and shoulders.— Smith said he did not see the horses turned out of the yard. They had great difficulty in keeping horses' shoulders free from sores during the hot weather. The magistrates fined Smith 40s. and costs in the first case and 20s. and costs in tho second, and the drivers Hunt and Ludbrook were fined in the costs. Christopher Murphey, Earl street, was charged with working a horse in an unfit state on the 22nd July. Harry Whitney,Arpley street,was summoned for causing the animal to be worked. Inspector Taylor stated that he saw Murphey driving a horse on the date in question. The animal was lame on the off fore leg and was travel- ling on three legs. Inspector Gibson of the R.S.P.C.A. stated that the animal was suffering from a severe sprain. Whitney was fined 10s. and costs, Murphey be- ing fined in the costs. THEM'S FROM A WARRINGTON WORKSHOP. At the Borough Police Court on Saturday, John Barnes, Ervrood-street, was sentenced to a month's imprisonment for stealing a box containing a set of coffin fittings, value 425. (id., from the shop of Mr. Edward Connor, Sankey-street. Superintendent Monaghan said a domestic servant named Hudson who was cleaning saw the prisoner take:the box containing the fittings from the cupboard and decamp. She followed him but he ran into Golborne-street and disappeared. He afterwards went to Mr. Massey, under- taker, and offered the fittings for sale. Mr. Massey retained them and said he would see the prisoner later. Being suspicions he'communicated with Mr. Connor with the result that the prisoner was arrested.— On Monday Barnes was again brought before the magis- trates, charged in two instances,lwith stealing a three- foot rule, and a saw and oil-stove the property of Mr. Edward Connor.—Superintendeat Monaghan said that the rule was left safe by a Henry Nixdorf who subse- quently missed it. It was found that the prisoner had sold it at a second hand dealers shop. The saw and the oil- stove were also missed and it was discovered that the prisoner had also sold these.—Prisoner, who pleaded poverty, was sent to gaol for one month.

THE" STRAND MAGAZINE "AUGUST NUMBER. The "Strand Magazine" for August keeps up worthily the excellent reputation it has earned as a budget of interesting miscellaneous reading. Amongstthe contents this month is an illustrated article, entitled" Painters of the Sea," which embraces examples of the-work of J. C. Hook, R.A., H. S. Tuke, Napier Hemy, W.L. Wyllie, etc., with a description of their methods. "The Man in Moccasins" is a short story by James Barr. NV. NV. Jacobs is responsible for "The Boatswain's Mate," which is as good as any other yarn of his we have read. "The Humour of Cricket' will find many interested readers, and the illustrations (several from, Punch ") are very amusing. The" Portraits of Celebrities at Different Ages" in- cludes Mrs. Craigie (John Oliver Hobbes), Lord Lans- downe, and Miss Annie Hughes. The other contents consist of the usual ample supply of stories, a new feature—" Chronicles of the Strand Club," and an article on " Great grandmother's Cookery Books," with some interesting illustrations.

PRACTICAL ADVICE.

the man raised his head slightly, and looked Lt the children. They could see his eyes Ihining through the long hair that hung about de face.

"Hungry," he answered, in a voice betweeu a whine and a growl. Louise, the little sister, was frightened; the man's eyes reminded her of the wolf—the wolf that she had heard about, that met Little Red-

_ Ridinghood on the road as she was going to, see her grandmother—and so she was frightened. Away she ran, scampering down the road toward the village as fast as she could. Marie also was frightened; not so much as her little sister, but she did not like being left alone with the stranger, and so she followed the younger sister, not looking behind her until they were again hand in hand. Then both looked back; the man had not stirred from his seat on the log. "He is hungry," said Marie. "Yes, he is hungry," repeated Louise. "He must be very hungry," said Marie. "Yes, he must be very hungry," repeated little Louise. .

"It must be terrible to be so hungry," said Marie, standing motionless in the road, and still looking back. "Yes, it must be terrible," Louise repeated again, pulling hard at her sister to preveut. her standing still. "Suppose we give him some of our luncheon:* said Marie. "And what should we do at noon?" asked Louise, opening her basket and looking in, to assure herself that her bread and jam were safe.

"Don't you remember, the Sister told us if we helped others, we would be provided fort Let us give the man some of our bread." "But the plum-jam?" questioned Louise. "Perhaps he likes jam," said her sister. "So do I," half whimpered Louise. "And, then the good Sister told us, the other day, about Saint Elizabeth. Don't you iji remember ho W, when she gave her best cloak to a beggar, a e found another—a better one— hanging up n her room?" "But the beggar did not eat up her cloak; it was not like bread and jam." _ . . ..

"No, but if we give our- luncheon to the beggar, perhaps—perhaps at noon we shall find a better luncheon in our basket, just as Saint Elizabeth found a better cloak when her husband sent for her to come down and see the kings who had come to make them a visit." "Are you sure, Marie?" "No, not sure, but perhaps. Let U 8 try." "I wish you would say sure." " Sure!" said Marie. "Say it again!" exclaimed Louise. "Sure I" repeated her sister. "He shall have my luncheon, then; but must we go back? Let us put it down here, and them run. He will find it, like the birds." (To be continued.)

SCIENCE NOTES AND GLEANINGS.

THE ACTION OF COERA.VENOM.

An important communication on the action oil the human system of the poison of the Indian cobra has been published. While earlier investi- gators ascribed death from cobra-venom in most cases to paralysis of the respiratory centres, the author concludes that the main cause is a rise of blood pressure caused by the contraction of the minute arteries, which thus afford a barrier to the circulation.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
FLOWER CINEMATOGRAPHY.

Photography by means of the cinematograph has actually sliewn the growth of a flower. A bud which bursts into bloom in, say, sixteen days, is exposed to a camera every fifteen minutes during the sixteen days. When the pictures developed from the films are assembled in order in the moving picture machine the observer may see, to his delight, all in a minute or two, the gradual breaking of the bud—the blossoms open, close by night, and reopen in the morning, the leaves grow under the eye, the stamens peep from cover, and, finally, the full.blown flower.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
AN INSECT WHICH BROODS.

A remarkable case of an insect sitting on its eggs is recorded by Mr. F. P. Dodd in the Transactions of the Entomological Society of London. This strange departure from the normal habits of the insect race is exhibited by a species of bug. The female sits in a brooding attitude over her eggs for a period of three weeks. When the young begin to break through she retires an inch or so away, and remains there for some hours until the last egg is hatched. She then de- parts, leaving the young ones to take card of themselves. During the whole time of brooding, and till all the young are hatched, the mother eats nothing. It is thought that this brooding habit may be a means of protection against the attacks of ichneumon flies.

ELECTROLYTIC DEPOSITION OF ZINC.

The experiments of M. Cabran for obtaining rapid deposits of zinc show that the electrolyte giving the best results is composed of 1,200 parts lof zinc sulphate. 60 parts of sulphuric acid of the strength of 24 deg. Baurne, and 6,000 parts el water. With this electrolyte and densities of current varying from two to three amperes per square decimeter clear, solid deposits of zinc were obtained. The tension of current was 1.5 to 2-5 volts. In other experiments the tension extended to six amperes per square decimet.r, without injuring the quality of the deposit, the voltage being from nine to ten volts. Crystals of zinc were formed on the borders of the cathode, but this did not prevent its being covered with a very adherent deposit of zinc.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNTERFEITING SEA WATER.

Counterfeiting sea water has been effected with practically indistinguishable exactness. In- deed, it has been accomplished so successfully that the most delicate animals, such as sea anemones, have been kept alive in the artificial fluid for months together. Apparently there is no material difference between the imitation and the article provided by old Neptune. Sea water is a complicated mixture. Roughly speaking, it consists of 9E4 per cent, of fresh water plus 3!..- per cent, of mineral salts. Three-fourths of these salts are chloride of sodium, or common table salt, and the next largest constituent is chloride of magnesium. After these come sul- phate of magnesium, sulphate of lime, sulphate of potash, bromide of magnesium, and carbon- ate of lime. In addition to these substances sea water contains minute quantities of quite a variety of elements, including iodine, phos- phorous, and arsenic. It also contains some silver, copper, zinc, nickel, cobalt, iron, and gold.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ORIGIN OF THE HORSE.

The origin of the horse is an interesting and difficult problem. Professor Cossar Ewart points out that in post-glacial, as well as in pre-glacial times, there were several distinct species of horse. And it is extremely probable, he thinks, that some of the prehistoric species have con- tinued almost unaltered to the present day. There are now three distinct horses living. Theso are Prjvalsky's wild horse of the Gobi desert, the Celtic pony, and the Norse horse. Two of these at least have taken part in forming a number of British breeds. Other varieties are a long-headed, heavily-built horse with a straight profile, and another with a more or less pro- nounced Roman nose. Several Occidental and several African and Oriental varieties also occur. The English thoroughbred is a mixture of Ori- ental, Occidental, and African varieties. It is therefore probable that it includes in its ances- try several wild species. In other words the horse has had a multiple origin.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
PARASITIC PEAS.

The parasitic plant growing on its host, as, for example, the mistletoe on the apple, is an interesting botanical problem. The seed of the parasite sends out its radicles into the tissue's of the host, from which it is able to absorb ela- borated nourishment. Mr. G. J. Pearce has succeeded in the remarkable feat of making a pea plant grow as a parasite on a bean. The radicles of the' sprouting peas were inserted in the stems of strong young bean plants, and kept in place by plaster of Paris. The roots of the peas grew downward through the stalk of the bean, but in no case did they reach the level of the ground. These parasitic peas flowered and produced seeds which afterwards germinated. They were smaller than the nor- mal plants, but the reduction was more marked in the case of the stem and foliage than in the seeds. The seeds from this generation of para- sites were planted in bean stems as before, arid produced somewhat smaller plants than the parents. There was no true union between the root of the pea and the tissue of the bean.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
• THE ATMOSPHERES OF PLANETS.

Some time ago Dr Johnstone Stoney contri- buted to the Transactions of the Royal Society of Dublin an able paper on the atmospheres of planets, including that of the earth. From a series of closely reasoned mathematical and physical considerations it was concluded that the gases hydrogen and helium, which should normally exist in the air, escape the control of gravity, and go wandering off in space until attracted by some other sun or planet. Similar considerations applied to the moon shewed that neither air, water, nor carbonic acid gas could be retained by the moon to form a lunar atmo- sphere. Applied to Mars, again, Dr. Stoney's arguments contradict the popular view that the white caps at the poles are snow. For the figures suggest that Mars cannot retain water in its at- mosphere. And as regards Mercury Dr. Stoney's conclusions are that it has probably no water. In a less degree it is probable that it has no air. Its atmosphere, if it has one, is probably argon and carbonic acid gas.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ELECTROCULTURE OF PLANTS.

The .investigations of Professor Lemstrom, of Helsingfors, and others, tend to show that electricity exerts a great influence on the growth of plarts, and this view, says the Scientific American, is confirmed by the luxuriant vegeta- tion of the zone of action of that violent'elec- trical manifestation, the aurora borealis. The results of the professor's experiments are contra- dictory in some respects, and shew that the ad- vantage derivable from electroculture depends also upon other factors, such as temperature, moisture of air and soil, and the natural fertility and the manuring of the latter. The supply of water is of especial importance. Extensive ex- periments with potatoes, carrots, and celery re- veal increases in crop of from 30 to 70 per cent. Potted strawberry plants, in the greenhouse, produce ripe fruit, under electrical influence, in half the usual time. Small differences, possibly due to extraneous causes, appear when the direction of the current is reversed. Other flelc: experiments give increases of 45, 55, occasionally 85 per cent, for grain, and 95 per cent, for rasp- *berries, while cabbage, tobacco, flax, turnips, and peas grow better without electrification than with it. _

In what way is the growth of plants affected by electricity? Plants transform the energy of the sun's rays into chemical energy. Though the heat produced by the electric current may have some direct effect, especially in germination, the electrical energy supplied cannot, in general, re- plaoe or even .greatly reinforce the energy of sunshine, It is rather to be regarded as a stimulus to metabolism and all the vital pro- cesses. One of these is the capillary elevation of water, which is promoted by a positive electric current flowing upward. This is one possible explanation of the promotion of growth by elec- tricity, and though. in some cases the best effect is obtained by directing the positive current downward, or in the opposite direction to the assumed principal flow of sap, these exceptions may mean that more food is supplied by the leaves than is commonly supposed. Another possibility is an increase in activity in both leaves and roots. The electrical influence on the flow of sap, however, appears to be proved by the fact that electroculture is beneficial Only in connection Witt., an abundant supply or wate, According to kernry, there is also an electro- lysia of water within the plant, and further ex. periment may prove the existence of other elec- trical actions.

[ALL RIGHTS RESSRVED.]

THE DIFFICtIT SEX,

JOHN STRANGE WINTER (Author of " Bootle's Baby," " Harty." 4.0.).

Of course the difficult sex is Woman The other sex can be classified and tabulated, ac- cording to class and condition, with a fair ap- proach to certainty as to how they will act. On the whole, men respond to the pressure of cir- cumstance and reason with admirable precision, and can be more or less grouped and dominated by leading ideas : with them action is generally in some visible proportion to cause. Any ex. -ceptions do not count for much. It is not so with the more itneresting sex. With them—the difficult sex—instinct and the personal note is ever apt to swamp logic, reason. law, arithmetic—everything. One has only to sit on a few women's committees to realize the absurdity of trying to secure the co-operation of women, for any conceivable end, by mere logic or even self-interest. To say this, is not to east a sneer at my sex—it is detestable for any woman to do that, and thanks to the pitiless justice of Time, unjust sneers never fail to damage the utterer eventimily. And it is no libel on,my sex to point out that we are prone to act on instinct, or emotion, before reason and fact assert their sway. But this tendency un- Aloubtedly makes perfect co-operation or organ- ization among women either impossible or ephemeral, and goes far to account for the com- parative failure of many feminist movements. It is wise to allow for it in all demands upon .our sex.

Nevertheless, there is no denying that the influence of women is practically all pemoiing. If they are not the stronger set, they alwoys colcur and often dominate it, and therefore it IM of the highest importance that their power shall be guided by right thinking, which alone can produce right action.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
DECADANCE A FALLACY.

Whatever woman may do by choice or nem, pity outside the purely domestic sphere which constitutes her natural domain, it will always :remain true that " the hand that rocks the cradle rules the world," /Ind nothing can trans- cend the power of the goad wife and mother. When I read violent and sensational diatribes against the women of our day—which suggest that we have all eagerly sunk into appalling wickedness and riotous immodesty—l men billy run through the hundreds of women and girls of all classes whom I know and mix with, to see how the cap fits. What do I bud ? I find almost every other hind, of woman except the one so luridly drawn as the typical woman of to-day ; I find the vast majority willing slaves to a mild conventionality. and so far from realizing the picture that the smallest approach to it on the part of anyone known to them, even by name, causes horrified comment ; I find many noble women patiently doing their best to bring brightness and light to others ; I find scores of lovable wives and mothers obviously filling their -positions with no small success ; I find few indeed at whom the finger of scorn could justly be pointed, or for whose inefficiencies and faults ordinary charity would not find much excuse.

It is ridiculous and mischievous to talk as though, because there ere many whose follies make them conspicuous, women in the mass are hopelessly decadent to-day ; there probably never was a time when so many women were Cfignged in good works, end filled with high ideals, as at the present time. It may be true also tglat there never was a time when women had so much,liberty and independence, and were so surrounded by alluring opportunities and temptations to misuse their privileges. But you cannot have freedom without risks ; privileges invariably bring abuses in their train, and those who are revelling in freedom are only too apt to overlook the price it involves—that of giving greater liberty to the powers of • evil as well ot good—and to let abuses become scandals before raising their hands to purity their surround- ings.

TITE WAIL OF TUE MALCONTENT. It must be remembered that the powers of Evil are always more rapid in actiou than those of Good—it is their compensation for not being so permanent. Evil always becomes unbearable, even to itself, before long ; while Good is never self- destructive. Its great let-down is that it is not self-assertive, and is too content to rely on its inherent quality .

The one thing needed, in these pushful days, to check the apparent prevalence of undesirable habits and customs, is that the millions of clean- living, well-meaning women among us shall be roused to exercise their due influence, and be less ready to let the tune be called by those whose lives and motives are less pure than their OWII.

- It is natural that malcontents shoal be more demonstrative than contents ; and if the con- tents allow them all the say, who can wonder if they convey the impression that contentment is non-existent ? Yet it is not the dissatisfied woman as a rule who can do useful work, or help any cause forward. The power rests with the pure and wholesome, and it is only when they will not exert it that the imperfect can get a show at all. How many goe,d causes, how many estimable societies, languish simply be- cause their most energetic workers are not their soundest and most single-minded members, but often their most self-interested and least esti- mable !

It is very difficult to get satisfied people, whether men or women, to interest themselves as keenly for the general good as those who have suffered, either justly or unjustly, and therefore ;ere often deeply biassed ; and, between the twn, it is not to be wondered at that the keen-sighted, ambitious, or self-seeking are apt to get the box seat in every movement.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
SATE 1:9 FROM OTR FRIENDS.

The rapid growth of evils in our midst, both in great matters and small, is slowly causing the soundest minds to awaken to the need of fundamental changes, and takes the curious form of a demand for restraint from the very quarters where liberty has been valued most ! The effects of organised effort, which past freedom has made possible in every direction, are being slowly met by a deep demand for in- dividual freedom from organised tyranny ; and we see on all sides a tendency to clamour for " freedom to negotiate !" Herein is a great op- portunity for " the difficult sex i" Let every good woman realise the greatness of her real power as an individual, and teach every girl that she is of infinite importance as an in- dividual, but comparatively ineffective in rigid co-operation. and many of the real evils of the day would steadily become less obstrusive and demonstrative.

When one looks below the surface of things, one cannot but see that the power of the best women among us is continuously exerted in a negative fashion. Can any reasonable thinker suppose that if female suffrage, for instance, was desired by happily married women, that it woul d not be granted as quickly as the methods of the Constitution would allow?

Does anyone suppose that divorcees, faddists, prudes, and fanatics either can or will present fair pictures of the world around them ? Let those who are not of these imitate their in- terested vigour, and hold up their own moderate lives and views with equal insistence, and where undoubted evils are found to exist set their faces more determinedly against them, and we shall soon see a more wholesome state a things around us.

If the best minds do not diligently strive for freedom of thought, of action, of labour. can they complain if interested minds organize restrictions which tend to enthrall them ?

In the Good Old Days.

Sir,—As bearing on the question of the mental alarm caused through motor-cars moving at speed in country districts, the deserted condition of our main roads is hardly appreciated by the generality of people. Travelling recently, on a drenching day, from London to Oxford and back, my motor neither met nor passed a dozen vehicles in the whole double distance beyond Uxbridge, except in the few towns on the route. For twelve miles straight-on-end only one cart was seen. Our through traffic for 50 years has been so entirely absorbed by railways that two whole generations have grown up looking upon roads as constructed and main- tained mainly for local purposes and for pleasant ease, but not for real use. A wet day empties them like a Sunday. A very different condition of things prevailed in 1831. The Post Office Directory of that date shows the daily timing from London of 51 Coaches bound for Bath, wet or fine, and of course the same number ran from Bath to London. This means more than 100 coaches in the 21 hours, therefore one must have passed any given spot about every quarter of an hour, to say nothing of fly- waggons, private carriages, and carriers' vans. These remarkable figures are, however, nearly doubled on another route. As many as 91 four-in-bands left London daily for Oxford and beyond, along that now deserted highway upon which the rain-swept motorist of to-day travels in absolute solitude. The roads in 1831 must have been highly "dangerous ! " A coach-and-four swinging along at an average pace of 10 miles an hour, up hill and down dale, , may be more picturesque and musical than a motor, but Inone the less it would give slender opportunity for such casual neglect of the rules of the road as obtains in the present day on the part of all drivers of horses, and it would afford little safety for children using the road as a playground. A coach travelling at 12 miles an hour 1 is less under control than a motor-car going at twice the pace. When this complete mastery over scar is more clearly 'understood, and when our minds are attuned to higher average speeds than that set by a trottting horse, we shall sensibly recognize that careful rapidity is not in itself a peril, that roads are important arteries of com- munication, and that it is for the general good that they should not any longer lie idle, dusty and neglected, but should again be well surfaced, busy, and alive with the pulse of progress under its most modern form—the throb of the swift and universal motor-car.—l am, sir, yours, &c., PIONEER. Midsummer, 1905.

===== 0002601_19050819_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
Ratepayers and the Markets.

Sir—Some time ago correspondence took place in your paper as to the condition ot the markets. No apparent benefit has accrued so far as concerns the matters com- plained of except perhaps in the way of police super- vision. It may be said here that one of the complaints had to do with the method of dealing with the refuse of the markets such as rotten fruit in the one market and the wasted fish in the other. It was very strongly pointed out that leaving rotten fruit lying about where poor little children could easily get to it constituted a danger to the health of those little ones, and that through the sating of such decayed fruit, disease might possibly be spread. Another complaint dealt with the emptying of fish bins, improper cleansing of these bins, along with faulty drainage &c., b3ing given as reasons to account for the objectionable smells more or less always conspicuous round the fish market. The unclassified arrangement of the stalls in both markets was another subject of comment. Besides the public correspondence drawing attention to these matters a letter was also written to the Chairman of the Markets Committee urging him to take some steps to remedy things. But that gentlemen wrapped himself up in official dignity—l was going to say ignorance. I would like to say that we require conscientious men in the Town Council and on the various Committees, not men over-burdened by the sense of their own importance. It appears to bean unwritten law among the members of I the Markets Committee that on no account must they be unfortunate enough to visit the markets which they are supposed to know such a deal about. Certainly some of the members have an unaccountable horror of being seen near the market area. This brings me to the subject of this present letter. I An application has been made by a local firm of auctioneers (Messrs. Vane and Co.) for a stand on ' Saturdays in the portion of the market (partly covered but mostly uncovered) bordering on Queen-street. The stand required does in fact run along the wall of the offices of Messrs. Vane and Co. The application was refused and for what real reason I should very much like to know, as no doubt woukl also many others. It was stated at the meeting of the Town Council when the application came up for consideration that that portion of the market applied for was primarily and specifically intended for farm produce only. But this was afterwards virtually denied. But even if it was so intended the space applied for is unoccupied and might as well be let. The stalls are pretty well overcrowded. A stall, however, is not what Messrs. Vane and Co. desire so they will not add to the overcrowding in this direction. The stand Messrs. Vane and Co. require is a vacant space away from the stall area altogether, This can be seen by personal inspection and if inspected on a Saturday night it would also be seen that there is plenty of room for a sale to be held that would not inconvenience any other party. A space of about 22 by 7 yards was vacant last Saturday night just outside the offices of Vane and Co. To say that this portion of the market is overcrowded is directly contrary to the actual state of affairs. Why rag-tag auctioneers (if one can call them auctioneers), should be allowed lighted stalls to sell all sorts of rubbish and a respectable firm of local auctioneers (fully qualified) refused a stand in some vacant space apart entirely from the infested area, is past my com- prehension. It has been mentioned in the reports of the Town Council meetings that Messrs. Vane and Co., sell furniture and other household goods. For sales of this deecription it is well that they should be held on the day most convenient to husband and wife (and in- tending husbands and wives) and others to attend. By thus allowing these sales to be held on a Saturday (which is a recognised market day) four advantages would accrue. First the public would be benefited, secondly, the firm would be benefited, thirdly the rate- payers would reap a benefit and, fourthly, it would be a benefit because it would do away with the great heap of refuse deposited alon,g Messrs. Vane and Co's., offices. This removed and a danger to the children (who scrum- mage in the refuse for fruit), would be considerably lessened.

As ths matter stands at present two sales are being held each Monday, thus one firm competing directly against the other—the possible outcome of this may be that one of the firms will quit the market altogether and the ratepayers will be the losers. Why could not Messrs. Vane and Co., have been allowed to continuo their Saturday sales without interruption ? It is a downright shame and injustice that the firm should be debarred from holding their sales on a proper market I day when the space is waiting for an occupant. I I should have thought that the recognised market days were the proper days for all sales. If not what are market days for? If one firm chooses Monday as their best day why should not another firm chooso Saturday? The space required is as vacant on a Saturday as it is on a Monday or Tuesday. I trust others will voice their opinions on this matter that thereby the Markets Committee will be influenced to grant Messrs. Vane and Co.'s application. I think an injustice has been done and for this reason I take the matter up. Fair play to all and no favour to one more than another. Yours,

The Examiner, Saturday, August 19, 1905.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Grlmsdale—Birtles.

Christ Church, Padgate, was the scene of a very pretty wedding on Tuesday afternoon, the contracting parties being Miss Ethel Birtles, daughter of Mr. Thomas Birtles, Brantwood. Fearnhead, and Legh House, Warrington, and Mr. William Henry Grimsdale, son of the late Mr. James Grimsdale, Ferry House, , Twickenham, Middlesex. Great interest centred in the event as both the bride and bridegroom are well known in Warrington and district. Mr. Grimsdale, it will be remembeied, was formerly electrical engineer and tramways manager to the Warrington Corporation, and left the town on obtaining a more important , appointment at Portsmouth. A largo congregation I assembled to witness the wedding, the pretty church being well filled. While the guests were striving, Mr. F. Blackwell, who presided at the organ, played some choice selections. Promptly at two o'clock the bride entered the church, leaning on the arm of her father, and as she walked up the aisle attended by her bridesmaids the organist rendered the " Bridal March "from " Lohengrin " (Wagner). The officiating clergyman was the Rev. J. W. Isherwood, of Shrewsbury (formerly of Warrington) who was assisted by the Rev. W. Pollock-Hill, Vicar of Padgate. The bride, who was given away by her father, looked charming in a dress of white mouseline-de-soie, with Brussels lace yoke and fichn, and a long sash of chiffon. She wore a veil of Brussels net and wreath of real orange blossom amid carried a lovely bouquet of lilies of the valley and orchids which, together with A diamond ring, was the gift of the bridegroom. The bride's gift to the bridegroom was a set of gold cuff links. The bridesmaids were Miss Dorothy Browne (niece of the bride) and Miss Grimsdale (sister of the bridegroom). Their dresses were of white silk eolienne with embroidered chiffon yokes and fichus and long sashes, and they wore lace hats, trimmed with mauve hydrangeas, with chiffon strings. They carried beautiful shower bouquets of mauve sweet peas and their ornaments were pearl pendants, the gifts of the bridegroom. Mr. H. A. Grimsdale, brother of the bridegroom, discharged the duties of best man. At the conclusics)...of the ceremony the wedding party left the church to the strains of Mendelssohn's "Wed- ding March." The reception was held at Brantwood and was largely attended, and when the guests were assembled on the lawn the scene was a bright and pleas- ing one. Later in the day the newly-married couple left for Scotland where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was of biscuit coloured crepoline trimmed with mauve and Paris lace,and she wore a mauve hat trimmed with roses. Mr. and Mrs. Grimsdale were the recipients of many handsome presents. For some years the bride has been actively associated with the work at Houghton Green Mission and the clergy and congregation expressed their good wishes by presenting her with a Bible. MR. CHAMBERLAIN AND LABOUR M.P.'S The St. Helens Attack—Mr. A. Henderson's Reply.

In the Iron Founder's INlonthly Report for August Mr. Arthur Elanderson, M.P.' in his • Parlia.ment Lry Notes " alludes to Mr. Charabarlain's attack on Labour representatives in the course of his St. Helens speech in June.

Mr. Chamberlain then said: "I believe I have behind me the mass of the working people. (Chesrs.) We shall see—l don't mean at the next election—l mean whenever the issue is brought home clearly at the one great issue. Then, I believe, that without regard to panties the great mass of the working people will ba on my side. It is said—have you considered that the representatives of Labour are all against you? Who are the representatives of Labour? (Cheers). Whom do you mean? Do you mean those who call themselves by that name, and who sit in Parlia- ment with the assistance of a political party? Do you mean the men who are most excellent politicians but who are very often bad Labour representa- tives? Did these men represent you in the time of the Boer War? ('No.') Every man was against you and against the war, and would have lost the confidence of our Colonies rather than continue the war. Did they represent you in regard to Home Rule? They represent some, no doubt. Did they represent you when the Compensation Act was before Parliament? ('No.') Can any of you tell me of any one of them who gave me any assistance?" _

Mr. Henderson's comment on the above is as follows: "One of our members at Preston has written, calling my attention to the latter part of this statement, which has reference to the attitude of the Labour Member towards the Workmen's Compensation Act, 1897, when I before Parliament. My correspondent very properly I considered tile point of vital interest to working men. i He has invited my opinion upon it, and though I fail to I see why I should defend the conduct of representatives of Labour seven years before I entered Parliament, I will endeavour to put my correspondent and our members in possession of the facts. Mr. Chamberlain asked his St. Helens' audience if they could name one Labour Member who gave any assistance on the Compensation Bill. From the oflicial I report I find that Messrs. Broadhurst, Burt, Woods, and Fenwick supported the second reading by speeches. True the Labour Members supported amendments having for their object the securing of compensation for injury to , health, for preventing contracting out, for bringing under the scope of the Bill agricultural labourers and shop assistants, for securing compensation when acci- dents began instead of after the first fortnight. Hal they not supported such amendments, in our judgment, they would have neglected the workers' interests. It is easy for party politicians to indulge in cheap sneers and play to the gallery by making statements without due regard to the facts and which are often out of harmony with their own previous declarations. In the present instance we have only to ask our readers to note the following extracts from speeches made by Mr. Chamberlain, which go to show how easy it is to im- pose upon the credulity of the people for the purpose of making a little party capital. St. Helens, 1905. Birmingham, 1900. Yorkshire Post, June sth. "The Times " October Ist. " Did they represent you "I willingly admit the when the Compensation Act assistance given to me by was before Parliament? Can Mr. Abraham (`Sfabon '), any of you tell me of any and I think by Mr. Woods one ny o mu and perhaps others, whom v I do not remember at the assistancef thern present time.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Warrington on Saturday secured a much needed two points in the South West Lancashire League table. Their opponents were St. Helens Rees and the match had one of those sporting finishes which if they occurred more frequently would make cricket the most attractive of games from the point of view of the spectator.

St. Helens batted first and recorded 70 runs. War- rington's task being apparently an easy one. It is with confidence that Hawtin and Podrnore opened the home innings but disaster followed disaster in rapid succes- sion and eight wickets were down for 33 runs. At this stage Hampson joined Thomrson and a determined stand was made. Both bat3mea played carefully and the rate of run getting was slow.

The arrears were steadily wiped off when at 86 Thomp- son had t, retire to the pavilion having scored 18 CLUB. The value of his innings cannot be over estimated, He went in at a very critical period and by careful play altogether altered the complexion of the game. When he departed the spectators did not know what to expect as the remaining player Bennett, is more successful as a bowler than a wielder of the willow.

Hampson was pretty well " set " and as Bennett approached the wicket Warrington's " stamper " by signs impressed upon him the necessity of his end up. Bennett disregarded the injunction and the first ball which he received shaved his wickets. Hampson then entertained the attack and the score was carried to 69 when Bennett was called upon to face Gregory's deliveries. He stonewalled the opening balls but one rising a little was apparently tipped by him into the hands of a helder behind the wicket. A confident appeal was made and before the umpire conld give his decision Bennett was departing for the pavilion. To the surprise of all the batsman was ruled not-out and Hampson immediately afterwards made the winning hit , the innings closing for 77.

In the bowling line Podraore was the most prominent taking 5 wickets for 17 runs. The remaining batsmen fell to Mason who has 28 runs scored off his deliveries. Mason performed the "hat trick."

Bowling.

Local representatives of the two great political parties indulged on Saturday in a friendly contest on the bowling green attached to the Conservative Club, the result being the discomforture of the Liberal repte- sentatives, who were no match for their Tory opponents Out of twenty single games played the men from Bewsey-street won only four, while two of their repre- sentatives, J. E. Warren, the captain, and W. Leigh failed to score at all. In the doubles the opposition fared even worse and a total majority of 104 points separated the teams.

The fourth of the series of mains organized by the Warrington and District League was commenced on the Sankey Chapel green on Saturday and concluded on Tuesday. Close and exciting contests were the order throughout. The most attractive game was witnessed in the semi-final between A. Gleave and J. Jolley, each a member of the Bull's Head team. Gleave, a young and promising player, opened confidently and ran to 6-3 His opponent then secured the jack and carried the score to 11-6. The parties next peeled at eleven and again at 12 when Gleave won by the odd point. in the final, Gleave had an easy victory over J. Roberts of Fe arnhead game.

To-day the Leine fixtures will be resumed. An in- teresting struggle is promise I between the Horse and Jockey and Chapel House temns and the od,ls are in favour of a victory for the Zrst-narned club who operate on their own green. The Cemetery men visit Penketh Park and are expecting to claim the honours of the encounter.

Cycling.

- _ The picturesque resort of Outten Park was the destination of the Warrington Cycling Club on Saturday and the weather being fine, though dull, there was a large turnout. The couuter-attraction of Acton Bridge proved too strong for the majority of the members and quite a large contingent stayed there for the afternoon. The faithful few, however, competed the fall journey and were amply compensated for the exertion, the ride through Delamere Forest being delightful.

On Tuesday a dozen members turned out for the run to Grimsditch via Daresbury and spent a very enjoyable evening. On Wednesday evening the final for the silver cup was held on the Sankey Chapel House bowling green. Some 40 members assembled and exciting con- tests ensued. The cup was won by Mr. J. Redfern, tho other prize winners being Mr. Melton and Mr. W. Whittle. The remainder of the evening was upent in a convivial manner. During the proceedings Mr. W. H. Bellian presented the cup and prizes in connection with the main.

Football. iNtany Warrington people seem to be a little in the dark as to the operation of the recently inaugurated Northern Union League rules. The principle is similar to that which regulates County cricket, the champion- ship resting with the club which has the greatest percentage of matches won. All the matches are either League or Cup contests, so that there will probably be just the same keen competition. The main difference in the constitution of the League is that all the clubs do not play one another, but have fixture lists arranged to suit their own convenience, with a minimum, how- ever, of ten home and home engagements with clubs which were recognised last season as first class.

1 The Warrington men had their first practice on Wed- nesday evening before a good crowd. A match was played between mixed teams, the first team forwards playing before the A " team backs and vice versa. Among the prominent players engaged were Hallam, Isherwood, Dickinson, Fish, Kenyon, R. Bite and Brookes. There was a tight game, Fish securing the , only try. He has done a fair amount of running daring the close season, and though heavier he showed surpris- ing pace on Wednesday evening.

The Widnes football team commence practice this Saturday, when it is anticipated that there will be a full turn out of players. The players have agreed to accept the same wages as was paid them last season for the first month of the coming season. Afterwards the rate of pay will depend largely on the financial outlook.

A young heifer broke loose in the Bradford Corpora' tion slaughterhouse on Monday and bolted with eight yards of chain fastened to its.ne9k. .James B.ooth got his legs entangled in the chain in his efforts to recap- ture the animal and was thrown to the ground. He received injuries to his head which resulted in his death a few hours afterwards.

A DISASTROUS COLLISION.

County Court Action at Warrington.

A case of considerable interest was heard by his Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C., at the Warrington County Court on Thursday. The action arose out of al collision which occurred in Chester-road on the 31stl December last between a van driven by a servant of Mr. John Babbitt, baker, Ellesmere-road, Stockton Heath, and a carriage float driven by Mr. Fowles, jun., farmer, Hatton. Mr. Rabbit claimed £5 us. 4d. damages sustained by him, he alleged, through the negli- gence of Mr. Fowles. In reply to this claim Mr, Fowles denied negligence on his part and counter- claimed £lOO damages for personal injuries and loss, the accident being occasioned, he alleged, by the neglig- ence of plaintiff's driver.—Mr. H. Lindon Riley, in- structed by Mr. A. Browne appeared for the plaintiff defendant being represented by Mr. Cuthbert Smith, instructed by Mr. T. S. Steel. Mr. Riley stated that on the 31st December last, a bread van driver by the plaintiff's servant was coin- ing into Warrington from the direction of Chester and the defendant was driving a carriage float in the opposite direction. Plaintiffs driver had neared the end of the road and was driving slowly when defendant drove from behind a tram car and dashed into him with such force that both ponies were thrown down. The off wheel of the bread van was shattered, almost the whole of the spokes being displaced, and the van greatly damaged. Mr. Rabbit promptly sent in notice of his in- tention of sending in a claim but Mr. Fowles was so seriously injured the proceedings were delayed. Herbert Dickinson, Oliver street, driver of the bread van, described the accident. Defendant he said was driving at a furious rate. There was plenty of room for the defendant to have passed had he kept on his right side of the road. In answer to Mr. Smith : He was sitting on the front of his van and held the reigns tight. He was a careful driver and had never run over anyone. His Honour: Has anyone ever run under you?

Witness : Yes, sir. Further cross-examined witness stated that Fowles kept on his right side of the road as far as he possibly could but his pony appeared to bs running away. Mrs. Vanda 'Wood said that on the 31st December she was in her yard in Chester-road, and witnessed the accident. Mr. Fowles was thrown under his horse and badly kicked. The driver of the van could not have done anything to avoid the accident, and was in no way to blame. Joshua Starkey, signalman, employed by the London and North-Western Railway,stated that he was on duty in the Wilderspool signal box when he noticed Mr. Fowles drive past. The horse was very wild and rest- less and took fright at a tramcar and ran away. William Speed, car driver, employed by the Warring- ton Corporation, gave similar evidence and evidence in support was also given by P.C. Cox and the plaintiff. Mr. Cuthbert Smith stated that the defendants story was that Dickinson was driving at a rapid pace and never attempted to pull up in fact the reins were hang- ing loose. He was not looking where he was going and driving along the centre of the road dashed into Mr. Fowles. Moreover according to the plaintiff's witness the collision was the result of a pure accident and he submitted that Mr. Rabbitt therefore had no claim. Not only was there no evidence of negligence against Mr. Fowles but he would produce evidence of negligence on the part of Dickinson. He asked his Honour to non- suit the plaintiff. His Honour declined to do so. After consultation with Mr. Cuthbert Smith, Mr. Riley announced that the parties had agreed to with- draw the claim and counter claim with no order as to costs. His Honour pointed out that if the case had been proceeded with and the defendant had failed he would have been involved to a great extent in costs. It was a great risk to take and defendant had exercised a wise discretion-

Wycliffe P.S.A.

There was a meeting of this society on Sunday. Mr. Nelson presided and spoke upon "Is it well " a good deal had been said and done to improve and better us dnring the 20th century much had been accomplished in science, art and skill but the great question was " Is it well morally and spiritually ?" Let them all try to imnrove themselves in those respects.Mr.G. Fairbrother of Bolton spoke upon "Life with all its great wonders, the natural animal and spiritual." They stood he said, between the great eternity of the past and the great eternity of the future. Life was theirs, to use well for time and eternity for man and God. Mr. W. H. Wallington sang" The Glory Song "with good effect, the men joining most heartily in the chorus. He also rendered" The crown of Glory "in a pleasing manner. Mr. .1. Owen and M. G. Smiley also took part in the meeting. The Hon. secretary Mr. B. Farrar heartily welcomed one new member. The savings club received 27 depositors. It was announced that prize lists would be ready on Sunday next.

TERRORS OF THE TRAIN. If ever I go to a dinner, or to a reception, or to a theatre (writes Mr. Peter Keary in "Pearson's Weekly my whole enjoyment is spoilt becausel know that wheKt we are coming out, and the crush comes, lam going t° step on some lady's dress and rip the thing to bother" ation, and be called a "stupid brute," and feel like eneci That upsets a sensative nature like mine. I apologies are are useless. The offer of safety pins, whic took to carrying, I abandoned when one lady told In° that for two ordinary or common or garden pins ell would box my ears. I have stayed at home lately, aU lam getting rather tired of it. I want to go out agao' and I must. Staying indoors this hot weather is °pre' sive and enervating. I have a garden party, a wedding and one or two theatres I should like very much tc! attend. For the next few weeks, therefore, I shoeld,. be extremely obliged to my lady readers if, in the event of their wearing a train dress (I think that is the riglif description), they would bunch it up and general!)'Pu it together when they are walking. • oft Because I may be there. I may be following behind. I am sorry to put ladies to this trouble and ine.,°ft venieuce on my behalf. I know that the train is lui9 training that I may admire it, and that the girl wh. ° it with me may envy it. But, honestly, I don't adintre,,d one little bit. I am fidgeting to two-step time,u-ra watching the toes of my shoes and nothing else. feeling like an amateur dancing master. Jam 10011911 for the weak threads and wondering where the riP Wbe be. And lam wondering also whether it wouldnlin; better to turn back and sit down and have a eet"
