Lady Cyclist Injured. Miss Lena Harris, 25, Howard-street, was riding her bicycle in Tanner's-lane, on Monday evening when a cyclist whose name has not been ascertained collided .. with her. The man, who was riding on the wrong side of the road, knocked the young lady off her • machine with the result that her elbow was dislocated. , P.C. Gilchrist happened to appear on the scene and !, after he had rendered first aid Miss Harris was taken to the Infirmary and was attended by the •house eurgeon. The worst feature of the case is that after he accident the man rode away without making any • inquiry as to Miss Harris's injuries. LADIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER THE CLOCK. BRI D N-STILEET.—Advt. The Warrington Rowing Club. • The President (Councillor T. J. Hulse) attended the: 4 second outing in connection with the above Club on Saturday afternoon, when a very pleasant time was spent. In addition to the President and Vice-Presi- dents, many local gentlemen accepted invitations and were rowed up the river from the Boathouse, 1 4, Howley, to Thelwall and back, a distance of ' about ten miles under ideal conditions. A short , meeting was held at the Pickering Arms, Thelwall, The Secretary read apologies for absence from Messrs. John Lee, S. Wakefield, J. Osborne and others. Mr. Arthur Booth (captain of the old Warrington Rowing • Club) in a brief speech referred encouragingly to the -4. revival of this branch of sport in Warrington, and to ' the success the club had attained during the season. The meeting which was quite enthusiastic wound up with Mr. Clement George's humorous sketch " The art of doing nothing." All of the party arrived at the • boathouse Howley, soon after six o'clock having . thoroughly enjoyed the afternoon. Beautiful Warrington Society. •-• In connection with the recently formed Beautiful , Warrington Society a circular letter has been issued • ~. setting forth the objects which the organization has in I. view and inviting new members. Several sub-com- mittees, it is stated, have been formed to deal with . -different branches of the society's work. Thus, one will promote the laying out of open spaces, planting ! trees, and encouraging the cultivation of gardens, and . the provision of window boxes. Another will aim at ' the preservation of features of interest, the building of artistic houses, &c., the cleanliness of streets, and the •T eneral improvement of the town. A third will , address itself to the problem of smoke abatement, ▪ 'while a fourth will seek to work, through the teachers, upon the rising generation in order to discourage destructiveness and mischief, and to: imbue the children with the desire to preserve and maintain all that is beautiful. It cannot be too clearly , • understood that it is not the intention of the Society to interfere with existing agencies, nor to take up a • railitant attitude upon any of these questions. It is ' hoped that the aims of the Society will appeal to . , Persons of all classes and of all opinions, and, as a consequence, a large and united band of workers will be brought together whose efforts will be directed to make Warrington beautiful. Claim Against Messrs. Crosfield. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday Mr. Thomas Halton brought an action against Messrs. Joseph Crostield and Sons, to recover Bs. costs incurred by plaintiff through defendants, with Mr. Browne, solicitor, and promised to be paid by the defendants' servant or agent. Defendants paid into Court £3 35., and counter-claimed £l5 17s. 11d. for goods supplied. Mr. A. Browne appeared for the Plaintiff, defendants being represented by Mr: Wingate • instructed by Mr. Evans. Plaintiff accepted the amount paid into Court in settlement of his Claim. He admitted the purchase of lime, &c. from the defendants, but held that it was Paid for by contract. —ln his evidence Mr. Halton stated that he had dealings with the defendants, and Ufl their behalf sold lime on commission. He sold Mr. -Newall, their engineer, a tank for SA 10s., and later /net Mr. Henry Roberts, defendants' managing direc- tor, and settled everything per contra the sum which ne owed the defendants being almost balanced by the amount which Messrs. Crosfield owed to him. The settlement was not put in writing, being only verbal. Wingate Saul produced correspondence which "he submitted showed that the settlement had not been 'entered into.— His Honour non-suited the defendants nPen the counter-claim. ABANDONING A CHILD. At Runcorn on Monday Thomas Gregory, (58) was summoned for abandoning his child Gertrude Gregory (11). Mr. .1. W. Knowles prosecuted for the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children. It was proved that the defendant had left the girl to run about the streets and take her luck. Another child fourteen years of age, had been sent to a home. The defendant was sentenced to a months' imprisonment without hard labour. RUNCORN Bo Y INJURED. A little boy named Richard Dunbobbin was knocked down by a milk float, belonging to Mr. Foster, dairy- man, in Church-street, Runcorn on Saturday afternoon. It is said that the lad, who, fortunately, is a deaf mute, ran straight in front of the horse, lie was placed in the vehicle, and driven to his parents'house in Rutland- land-street. Dr. Delaney examined him and found him to be suffering from shock, slight internal injuries and severe bruises. AFTER TWENTY-FIVE YEARS. At Runcorn, on Monday, Edward Boden, of Bentinck- street, was summoned by his wife. Alice Boden, for deserting her. The defendant pleaded guility. Mrs. Boden said she had been married twenty-five years. Her husband was foreman at Riincorn lay-by. He had deserted her because of a telegram that came from a woman at Smethwick, Birmingham. She opened the telegram, which stated. "What's wrong you do not write ?—Noll." They had words in consequence, and he left the house and had not been there since.—The defendant accused his wife of drinking and immorality, and said that twelve months ago,when he was injured et the lay-by, she put every article belonging to himself and the children in the pawusop. She owed £lO 16s. for rent and £3 Is. 9d. for coal.—The bench granted a separation order, the wife to have the custody of the children, and to receive 12s, 6d. maintenance.—The Defendant: Very geed, sir; thank you. —4-........ WILLING TO DO THE SAME. Among the many stories that Andrew Carnegie de- lights to tell of the canny Scot is one in which Lord Derby and a collier figure. It appears that while the latter one day was wander ing on certain land belonging to Derby the collier chanced to meet the owner face to face. Rig lordship inquired if the collier knew on whose estate he was trespassing. "Well, I've got no land of my own, and I'm like to walk on somebody's. Wheer did the' get it fro'?" asked the collier. "I got it from my ancestors," replied the Earl, good- taturedly. "An' wheer did they git it fro'?" queried the trespasser. "Why," continued Derby, humoring the collier, "they got it from their ancestors." "An' wheer did they git it fro' ? " " They fought for it.' Whereupon the collier put up his fists, and, squaring fup to the Earl, exclaimed: "Well, I'll feight thee c r it." _____.....— THE LANGUID SHOP GIRL Is a Victim of Anmmia ; the Cure is Dr. William's Pink Pills. Sold at medicine shops, If in doubt, send to Dr. Williams' Medicine Co., Holborn-viaduct, London, enclosing 2s. 9d. for one box. six for 13s. 6d. DREDGING THE MERSEY AND SHIP CANAL. The Ship Canal Company have furnished information to the effect that in the length of the river Mersey between Monks, Hall and Company's work at Bunk Quay, Warrington, and Howley Weir, 439,600 tons of material have been dredged, and have been deposited on land above the influence of the highest tides. During the year 1901, 73,180 tons of silt have been dredged from the length of the Eastham channel at or near buoy E 3 ; and 76,120 tons of silt have been dredged in the immediate approach to Eastham Locks. The whole of this material has been deposited at sea. In the length of the canal between Eastham Locks and Latchford Locks, 462,308 tons of silt have been raised by the dredgers. Of this quantity, 170,240 tons have been deposited at sea and 292,061 tons on the tipping grounds of the com- pany beyond the reach of the highest tides. At the mouth of the river Weaver, 9,507 tons of , silt and clay were dredged to form a turning basin, of I which 7,920 tons were deposited, at sea, and 1,397 tons ,on land. From the non-tidal portion of the canal (Latchford Locks to Manchester Docks) 107,830 tons of 'silt and 9,540 tons of rock, clay, and gravel were dredged and deposited at sea, in addition to the quantity dredged and deposited on land. The total quantity of silt dredged from the canal daring the year 1904 was 1,226.069 tons. In giving me this information, says the Acting Conservator, the Ship Canal Company state as their opinion that the whole of this quantity of silt and detritus would have eventually found its way into the estuary of the Mersey, had not the canal works been interposed. A timber wharf, 153 feet in length, has been con- structed on the southern bank of the canal, immediately above the Transporter Bridge. at Runcorn. Royal Assent was given on August 15th to the Man- chester Ship Canal Bill to authorize, inter Min, the raising of the level of the water in the canal between Eastham and Latchford and the construction of a dam across the river Mersey, near Warrington.

CHURCH DISESTABLISHMENT. An Election Manifesto for the People In view of the next General Election, the following Manifesto has been issued by the Executive Committee of the Churchmen's Liberation League : We stand for the principle of " A Free Church in a Free State." We desire to liberate the Church of England from the control of the State because we believe- 1. That State-control enfeebles, and tends to destroy the spiritual life of the Church, by encouraging her to lean on the support of, and to accommodate her teach- ing to, the Secular power. 2. That the Church of England, as a religious society, has a right to self-government, free from the interfer- ence of Crown or Parliament, and subject only to the Divine law. 3. That, in a country where there are many forms of religion, it is unjust and invidious that one should be maintained by the State in a position of privilege and pre-eminence. (We know that the sense of this injustice ! is keenly felt by Christians outside the Church of England.) 4. That the maintenance of an Established Church is harmful to the spiritual life of the nation; because it ' tends to foster a false conscience, and to make people think that National Righteousness consists in the legal establishment of the Church, rather than in the personal religion of individual men and women. 5. That the subjection of the Church to the State is an invasion of the Sovereign Rights of the Lord Jesus Christ as King of the Universal Church and Master of His own house. The Disestablishment of the church would, in our judgment, involve the following changes :- 1. The Sovereign would cease to be "Supreme Governor of the church." 1 2. The Bishops would no longer sit in the House of Lords, but the clergy would no longer be disqualified for sitting in the House of Cemmons. 3. The control of the doctrine,discipline, and worship 'of the Church would be withdrawn from Parliament. I 4. The Judicial Committee of the Privy Council I would no longer have authority in religious matters, and Ecclesiastical Courts would cease to possess coer- cive jurisdiction. 5. Private and Official Patronage in the Cure of Souls would be abolished. The Bishops would be no longer nominated by the Crown, but elected by the Church. So far, we have spoken of Disestablishment only. But it is not likely that the State (as represented by I Parliament) would consent to Disestablishment unless I

This is the second prize that this lucky little girl has won. I have received a very nice specimen of freehand drawing from Jessie Bond. 1 was very pleased with this, and wish other mem- bers want& folio sr this little girl's example. I hope many of you have sent me the pen and ink sketch and boxes, as I want to have a good selection to choose from. You must remembar that August 31st is the !fist day for receiving the lists of those competing for the prize watch, etc. I have had several very good lists in already; 1: would not be fair to tell you the largest number of names received, but you .must all do your utmost to obtain new membens before the end of this month. I wo lder how many of you have nice gardens? This is the time of year for mignonette, sweet peas, lavender, hollyhocks, and many of those old-fashioned country flowers. I will tell you what I intend to do a little later on, and that is to hold a flov.'er show amongst the members of the "Children's Corner." I shall give two or thrae rounds in prizes for the best sped- mous of certain flowers. At a certain time, which I shall tell you, you will have to give in your names to me, and then I shall let you know the seeds you will want. You will have to do all the work yourself—in fact, do all that is required until the flowers are sent in for the competition. I used to exhibit plants and get prizes, and very interesting work it was. Now, what do you all think of this idea? I would like you to write and tell me; also say what plants you would prefer to grow for plizes. I will give a prize to the first correct solution to this week's puzzles. 0 With all good wishes, from UNCLE NED. --4-- An "Ironclad" Keyless Lever Black Watch, marvellous timekeeper, dust-proof, high4st perfection, supplied by Messrs. There is nothing to pay, but splenuid prises to be gained. __....___ BREAD AND JAM. It happened in France. Two little girls were on their way to school one morning in the Aiummer-time. These little girls lived with their mother on the boundary of the village, and their school was ,in the village, so They had a long walk along a solitary road between their home and the "Sister's" school, as it was called, for the teacher was a Sister of Charity. Each of these little girls had a basket on her arm, and in each basket were large slices of white bread, stuck together with plum-jam. They were very fond of white bread made of wheat flour, ior the principal food it their home was black bread, which was made of buckwheat; and they considered white bread a luxury. Later in the year, each would have had a big rosy apple to eat with the bread, tnstead of jam, but the apples were not ripe—as yet they were hardly larger than cherries. These little children were not sorry, however, for they liked plum-jam much better than an apple even the ripest, rosiest apple. There was no danger the jam would soil their school-books, for they had none—their lessons were written by the Sister with chalk on a blackboard, and they had no need of books. . Hand in hand, these little girls were trudging along the solitary road, when, turning a corner, they saw before them a man, sitting on a log, with his head buried in his hands. The children were not much frightened; why should they be, by a man resting upon the side of the road? No one had ever harmed them. They could not see the man's face, but, thinking he must be some one they knew, they went on fear- lessly, stopping when they were opposite the stranger. The man did not stir, and they looked at him in silence for some seconds. "What is the matter? " asked Marie, the elder.
